cross A Tensor-Algebraic No-Go Theorem for High-Dimensional Photonic Greenberger--Horne--Zeilinger States Discovered with AlphaProof Nexus

Authors: Mario Krenn, Moritz Firsching, George Tsoukalas, Rishikesh Gajjala, Xuemei Gu, Swarat Chaudhuri

Abstract: Photonic GHZ states generated with probabilistic pair sources can be described by edge-coloured, complex-weighted graphs. In this representation, conditioning on an event with one photon in every detector turns the output amplitudes into coherent sums over perfect matchings, and generating an \(N\)-photon \(D\)-dimensional GHZ state is equivalent to constructing a monochromatic quantum graph. Here, we prove that no complex-weighted monochromatic quantum graph exists for any even \(N\ge4\) when \(D\ge N\). The proof, discovered through AI-assisted formal proof search in Lean using AlphaProof Nexus, constructs a weighted sum \(\mathcal F\) of amplitudes whose coefficients factor vertex by vertex. Summing over the colours at one fixed vertex reduces the problem to an \((N-1)\times N\) matrix of effective edge weights. For the graph amplitudes \(\omega\) and GHZ target \(\Delta\), the row--column imbalance yields \(\mathcal F(\omega)=0\) but \(\mathcal F(\Delta)\neq0\), contradicting the required equality \(\omega=\Delta\). Finally, we give a physical interpretation of the AI-discovered mathematical proof.

cross C-LiTE: A Lightweight Foundation Model for Cellular feature learning in Histopathology

Authors: Esha Sadia Nasir, Jiaqi Lv, Gozde N. Gunesli, Shan E Ahmed Raza

Abstract: Characterising individual cells is fundamental to computational pathology, as cellular morphology informs tasks such as tumour grading, mitotic assessment and immune profiling. However, most pathology foundation models are trained on tissue patches and capture cellular features only implicitly, while existing cell-level models are either restricted to specific cell populations or rely on computationally expensive architectures with hundreds of millions of parameters. Applying ViTs directly to small cellular crops also presents an architectural challenge: upsampling increases computational cost without adding morphological information and may introduce interpolation artefacts. To address these limitations, we introduce C-LiTE, a lightweight foundation model for cellular representation learning in H&E-stained histopathology, with only 22 million parameters. C-LiTE employs a native-resolution tokenisation strategy tailored to cellular inputs, processing 64 x 64 px cell-centred crops using a 4 x 4 patch embedding to produce a 16 x 16 token grid without image upsampling. To transfer visual knowledge from large-scale pre-training, we adapt the patch embedding weights of a pre-trained ViT-5-S using a pseudoinverse-based transformation, enabling weight initialisation at the finer patch resolution. We further assemble a pre-training dataset of 60 million cell-centred crops from TCGA, balanced across 19 cancer types and 16 tissue types. To our knowledge, this represents the largest public dataset for self-supervised pre-training on H&E images at the cellular level. We evaluate C-LiTE on seven public datasets spanning mitotic figure recognition, nucleus and cell-type classification, and immune cell phenotyping. Under frozen-feature evaluation, C-LiTE achieves a mean macro-F1 of 0.670, approaching CytoFormer (0.682) with approximately 30x fewer parameters.

cross A Control-Theoretic Approximation to Predictive Coding Dynamics

Authors: Ryan Fayyazi, Kyle Daruwalla, Mitra Javadzadeh

Abstract: Backpropagation remains the dominant method for training deep neural networks, but its reliance on non-local computations has motivated the search for biologically plausible alternatives. Two prominent frameworks, predictive coding (PC) and deep feedback control (DFC), offer distinct approaches to local learning, yet their relationship remains unclear. Here, we show that DFC arises as a local approximation to predictive coding dynamics near fixed points, with exact equivalence in shallow linear networks and systematic deviations arising with depth, nonlinearity, and distance from equilibrium. We characterize how this approximation, which we call Approx PC, deviates from PC in its activity dynamics, and show that these deviations can be mitigated by relinearizing the feedback controller and approaching the output target through a sequence of intermediate nudges. In networks trained on MNIST and FashionMNIST, Approx PC reaches task performance comparable to PC trained with the same nudges, and weights learned with it transfer directly to PC inference. Our results unify energy-based and control-theoretic perspectives on learning and open the door to applying tools from control theory to the training of predictive coding networks.

cross Online Temporal Rotations for 2 Bit KV-Cache Quantization in Video Diffusion Transformers

Authors: Ryan Lucas, Petros Prastakos, Rahul Mazumder

Abstract: Video diffusion transformers reuse key--value (KV) caches across denoising steps, which quickly becomes a bottleneck for long video generation. We find that quantizing temporally distant cache vectors leads to a large range and high quantization error, but that nearby video tokens in the KV-cache share a persistent pattern per channel. These patterns also vary across videos, motivating rotations that are adapted to each video's unique cache. We propose Temporal Rotation Quantization (TRQ), a method that rotates frames in the cache that are adjacent in time before quantization. For each block of temporally adjacent cache vectors, TRQ fits an orthogonal transform that maps the block's per-channel means to a vector that is close to a constant across channels. To keep the quantization format lightweight, we parameterize this rotation as a tree of $2 \times 2$ planar rotations, each stored with only two bits, followed by a fixed Hadamard transform shared across all blocks. The rotations are fit without gradients or changes to model weights, and we prove that a simple greedy fit is globally optimal for our 'mean-flattening' objective. The transforms are fit online for each new video, and we release fused Triton kernels to make this process efficient at inference. At 2-bit precision and matched compression, TRQ improves VBench subject consistency over the state-of-the-art method QVG from 90.38 to 92.02 on LongCat-Video-13B and from 86.33 to 87.61 on HY-WorldPlay-8B, as well as improving other metrics.

Zhao, Mike Zhou, Tong Zhou, Zhenpeng Zhou, Tina Zhu

Abstract: Beam is a sparse Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) model with 501.3B total parameters and 23.4B active parameters, specifically built and optimized for coding, reasoning, and agentic workloads. Beam delivers frontier level reasoning, while requiring 3-4x less inference compute than many models of its class. Pretrained on 23.8T diverse, curated, high-quality tokens, Beam features a 1M-token context window achieved through a novel two-stage midtraining phase, built on verifiable, correctness-anchored data. Post-training leveraged a large-scale, fully asynchronous reinforcement learning (RL) campaign across 10.5K GB300 GPUs, generating over 100M rollouts over 4 weeks of training. We believe this is one of the largest scale RL runs conducted by any open lab to date. Across our evaluation suite, capabilities continued to improve as we increased RL compute, with no sign of a plateau. Supported by deep hardware-model co-design, FP8/NVFP4 quantization, and a 1.93B-parameter speculative decoding drafter (DFlash 2), Beam maximizes serving throughput with efficient, flexible deployment. Averaged across conversation turns and harm categories, Beam refuses or pivots on 88.9% of harmful requests while complying with 81.5% of harmless requests, achieving the highest refusal/pivot rate on harmful requests among open-weight models on the Pareto frontier.

cross Flow Matching from Non-Gaussian Sources: A Theory of Efficient Sampling

Authors: Yuchen Zhou, Lili Zheng, Yuxin Chen, Gen Li

Abstract: Flow matching is a versatile framework for generative modeling that transports a source distribution to a target data distribution. A key advantage is its flexibility in the choice of source distribution, such as heavy-tailed and multimodal sources. Yet finite-step sampling guarantees for non-Gaussian sources remain limited. We establish a nonasymptotic convergence theory for Euler discretization of flow matching with linear interpolation. Suppose the source distribution on $\mathbb{R}^d$ has an everywhere positive, continuously differentiable density, finite total variance $\mathsf{TotVar}_{\mathsf{base}}$, and an $L$-Lipschitz score. Using the population velocity field and a suitable time grid, this sampler achieves total variation error at most $\varepsilon$ in $\widetilde{O}(Ld\mathsf{TotVar}_{\mathsf{base}}/\varepsilon)$ iterations, provided the target distribution satisfies a mild second-moment condition. These assumptions cover, among many examples, source distributions such as multivariate Student's $t$ distributions with more than two degrees of freedom and finite Gaussian mixtures with shared covariance, while accommodating target distributions with neither a smooth density nor bounded support.

Abstract: A central goal of humanoid robotics is for robots to perform the everyday work people do in homes and workplaces. cross Asuka-FM: A System-Architectural Co-Design Framework for Efficient Text-to-Image Pretraining on Consumer Hardware

Authors: V\'ictor Rodrigo Gonz\'alez, Pablo M. Olmos, Ignacio Peis, Alejandro Lancho

Abstract: Frontier text-to-image (T2I) foundation models predominantly rely on multi-billion parameter Diffusion Transformers (DiTs) pretrained across massive compute clusters, creating an accessibility barrier for academic laboratories. When training DiTs from scratch on single consumer GPUs (e.g., an NVIDIA RTX 4090 with 24\,GB VRAM), practitioners encounter three primary bottlenecks: quadratic attention sequence costs, Just-In-Time (JIT) compilation graph breaks induced by variable aspect-ratio bucketing, and an autoencoder throughput wall where frozen Variational Autoencoder (VAE) operations dominate step latency. cross Mind2Act World: A Diagnostic Benchmark for Embodied Mind, Physical Action, and Mind-Action Composition in Robotic Manipulation

Authors: Yuzhi Huang, Jie Wu, Fanding Huang, Junchao Lin, Weichen Zhang, Ziyi Xiong, Yang Li, Zhengwei Xie, Zhimin Wang, Zhen Liu, Jiazhen Huang, Yuzheng Zhang, Yuche Liu, Hongyi Yang, Chen Tang, Shixiang Tang, Hakan Aktas, Adam Wu, Duo Wu, Kangye Ji, Shuzhao Xie, Huanan Liu, Weijue Bu, Chenxin Li, Xinyuan Wang, Tianyi Bai, Yinan Mao, Jiawei Wang, Minglei Li, Heng Yu, Ehsan Adeli, Minchen Li, Yong-Lu Li, Cengiz \"Oztireli, Huazhe Xu, Chao Yu, Jing Zhang, Shanghang Zhang, Weitao Zhou, Wenwu Zhu, Cheng Chi, Jingyan Jiang, Zhi Wang

Abstract: Embodied intelligence aims to let robots perform complex physical tasks with human-like adaptability and reliability, which requires three complementary capabilities: history-based cognitive reasoning, precise and timely physical execution, and the composition of reasoning and execution through environmental feedback. Yet existing benchmarks either evaluate reasoning and execution within the same tasks or assess them separately without their composition, so they reveal little about whether a failure comes from reasoning, execution, or their composition. We introduce Mind2Act World, a diagnostic benchmark of 30 simulated manipulation tasks from five real-world application domains. Each task has three difficulty levels and two scene settings (Clean and Random), yielding 180 configurations. The tasks form three views: Mind tests decisions based on past observations, Act tests precise and timely execution, and Mind2Act combines both in closed-loop tasks where action outcomes guide later decisions. Comparing the three views shows whether a system lacks a capability or fails to compose capabilities it already has. A diagnosis harness further attributes the failed episodes of three representative systems to Mind, Act, or their composition and summarizes each system in a profile card. Our evaluation of multimodal agents, vision-language-action models (VLAs), and world-action models (WAMs) shows that competence in a single view does not carry over to Mind2Act tasks. Even the strongest agent succeeds in 52.7% of Mind and 39.0% of Act episodes but in only 12.0% of Mind2Act episodes. The strongest agent retains task state but struggles with sustained contact, whereas end-to-end policies execute practiced motions but retain little task state. These findings point to composing reasoning and execution through environmental feedback as the main open problem.

cross A new algorithmic approach for finding flat minima: critically damped Polyak stepsizes

Authors: Francesco Caporali, Jason M. Altschuler, Boris Hanin

Abstract: We present a new adaptive stepsize rule that biases gradient descent toward flatter minima in both theory and practice, and establish the first uniform-in-time guarantees for both sharpness and loss reduction for a broad class of locally quadratic objectives. Our critically damped Polyak (CDP) stepsizes multiply the classical Polyak rule by $4$ and a mild damping factor. The factor $4$ is critical in that it produces equioscillations around the manifold of minimizers $\mathcal{M}$ to leading order. Damping provably drives convergence to $\mathcal{M}$. CDP thus operates at the edge of stability by adapting its stepsizes to local curvature. Once the loss is small, its projected iterates follow gradient descent on $\log{\lambda_1(\nabla^2 \mathcal{L})}$ along $\mathcal{M}$ to leading order, paralleling the flatness bias of normalized gradient descent (Arora et al., 2022). Unlike normalized gradient descent, CDP step lengths shrink with the distance to $\mathcal{M}$, enabling convergence to an interpolating solution that locally minimizes sharpness at finite learning rate. We establish our theory for a broad class of non-negative losses with locally quadratic Morse-Bott structure near $\mathcal{M}$, capturing regular interpolation regimes in smooth overparameterized neural networks. Empirically, CDP stepsizes produce a flattening effect similar to that of Sharpness-Aware Minimization (SAM, Foret et al., 2021), reducing sharpness even in settings beyond our theory, including nonlinear teacher-student networks and fine-tuning of pre-trained LLMs.

cross Backtracking Agent Applications Security Incidents

Authors: Yicheng Cai, Taegyu Kim, Peng Liu

Abstract: Agent applications are increasingly deployed, yet agent-specific attacks pose real threats to them. Upon a security incident, organizations backtrack to identify the CEP (compromise entry point), remediate the damage, and prevent its recurrence. The main artifacts available for backtracking are trajectory logs, which record agent functionalities, and source code; app(lication)-specific function call logs are often fairly limited. However, trajectory logs are at the agent functionality abstraction layer, while the CEP candidates are function calls at the app code execution layer. Due to this semantic gap, the analyst needs to manually inspect the relevant code for each CEP candidate to infer its corresponding agent functionality. In this work, we present Origin, a backtracking tool for the analyst to submit a small number of queries to identify CEPs that existing tools cannot provide. Our approach poses two challenges: (1) define the intermediate abstraction that captures the mapping relationships between the two semantic layers; (2) determine the region-to-variable mapping for constructing the cross-layer graph defined in the intermediate abstraction. Each region is a specific group of agent functionalities. Origin addresses the challenges by (1) the cross-layer dependency graph (CLDG) that connects region-inferred functions at the semantic level to the variables in the low-level taint propagation subgraph, plus the associated query language, and (2) the region inference algorithm. Case studies on five popular agent applications across eight attack incidents show that an analyst using Origin correctly identifies all the CEPs.

cross Cost-Aware Agentic Feature Optimization System for Fleet-Scale Ranking Models

Authors: Chang Yang, Yao Xuan, Wendan Yan, Nhat Le, Jiang Liu, Longhao Jin, Zhen Peggy Yao, Alireza Vahdatpour, Djordje Gligorijevic

Abstract: Large Web platforms operate large fleets of ranking models, each selecting features from a shared pool of tens of thousands. cross AURA: AUdio-dRiven streaming Avatar

Authors: Nathaniel Cohen, Nicolas Dufour, Am\'elie Royer, Alasdair Newson, Patrick P\'erez

Abstract: Current audio-driven video diffusion models can synthesize realistic talking-head videos but generally require dozens of diffusion denoising steps over multi-second video chunks, making them unsuitable for streaming applications such as voice assistants and virtual avatars, where low-latency and online causal generation are required properties. AURA generates video chunk by chunk with a rolling KV cache, requiring only the audio of the current chunk to start generating, rather than a multi-second audio look-ahead like bidirectional models. It comes in two sizes: AURA 1.3B streams 576p video in real time on a single GPU (20.7 fps for a 16 Hz playback rate), making it suited to interactive avatar applications, and AURA 5B streams 720p video at 9.7 fps. Our two-stage distillation pipeline first aligns the causal student with the teacher, by training it to match the teacher's trajectories with fewer diffusion steps and a chunk-wise sliding window causal attention mask, then applies Distribution Matching Distillation (DMD) with backward simulation to close the remaining quality gap. On standard talking-head benchmarks, AURA 1.3B runs 23x faster than its own bidirectional teacher and reaches a quality comparable to TalkVerse at a lower resolution, and AURA 5B matches its TalkVerse teacher at 720p while running 17x faster. To support reproducibility and further research on streaming avatar generation, we release the weights of both models along with a dataset of 30K synthetic videos and their precomputed teacher trajectories.

cross Replication of Agentic Security Events: Audit and Investigation (ROGUE-AI)

Authors: Mengxiao Wang, Mahbub Alam, Kalyan Nakka, Nitesh Saxena

Abstract: Agents based on large language models (LLMs) can now delete files and send payments on behalf of their users. News media reports routinely describe failures of these agents (e.g., Replit's coding agent deleting a production database), but a report often only says what went wrong and rarely (in)validates the incident or finds its root cause. Incident catalogs collect these reports, and benchmarks record whether agents fail, but neither replicates an incident nor tests the cause its report might identify. In this paper, we present ROGUE-AI (Replication of Agentic Security Events: Audit and Investigation), a multi-agent framework that closes the above gap and rebuilds each reported incident offline together with a matched control that lacks only the blamed cause, and runs agents on both. Our results suggest that ROGUE-AI is able to reproduce the reported harm in 89% of its 108 attempted replications. Agents built on five current GPT, Claude, Gemini and DeepSeek models made the reported mistake in 18% to 33% of runs, against at most 1% without the blamed cause. Of the 72 incidents with real-world harm, 62 had no underlying attacker, and in these, agents did what their task asked for but missed an ordinary hazard, such as a database shared with the live site. Replications without an attacker caused more harm than replications of prompt injection for all five models. Defenses against prompt injection look for injected instructions, but these replications contain none. Further, a warning after each tool output that names the hazard and its consequence lowered harm for all five models, whereas a note of the same length that does not mention the hazard left most of the harm in place. Built from real incidents, the ROGUE-AI replications form a reusable audit framework that vendors can rerun before each new release.

cross Multi-institutional grading of Barrett's esophagus dysplasia without stain normalization using a pathology foundation model and multiple instance learning

Authors: Amirali Khosravi, Jaidip M. Jagtap, D. Chamil Codipilly, Catherine E. Hagen, Jason T. Lewis, Rickey E. Carter, Shahriar Faghani, Vani J. A. Konda, Fouad Otaki, Shajan Peter, Nicholas Shaheen, Sachin Wani, Prasad G. Iyer, Bradley J. Erickson

Abstract: Generative engines synthesize retrieved web pages into answers, recommendations, and rankings. Authors: Ana E. Sanchez Villalobos, Michael D. Quinn, Aakash Tyagi, Minh D. Luu, David Kebo Houngninou

Abstract: A passing UVM regression shows the design agrees with the reference model on what the scoreboard checks, not that it meets the specification. cross Realized versus Population Reliability: Anytime-Valid Self-Consistency Certification under Correlated Reasoning

Authors: Terence D. Agbeyegbe, Toneukarin G. Agbeyegbe

Abstract: Self-consistency certification treats repeated sampling from a language model as sequential evidence accumulation and stops when an anytime-valid e-process crosses a threshold, guaranteeing finite-sample Type I error control by Ville's inequality. Prior constructions assume sampled rollouts are conditionally independent. We show this assumption fails in practice: reasoning streams are positively correlated through shared reasoning prefixes or across-prompt heterogeneity, changing not merely certificate efficiency but the hypothesis certified. Modeling dependence via a per-unit latent state (the random-effects / intraclass-correlation structure) induces exchangeable, equi-correlated increments with correlation $\rho$. Correlation splits the single null into a realized null about the drawn belief and a population null about average model behavior, which coincide only when $\rho=0$. We show: (i) the standard betting e-process remains anytime-valid for the realized null, not the population null; (ii) read as a population-null test, a suitable mixture has asymptotic crossing probability at least $\pi=P(\mu(Z)>0)$, potentially exceeding nominal $\epsilon$; (iii) the within-unit mean's effective sample size saturates at $1/\rho$, so extra within-unit rollouts cannot restore population validity; and (iv) a prompt-level e-process restores population validity, with optimal allocation at one rollout per prompt under a pure rollout budget. Simulations and Claude Haiku 4.5 rollouts confirm this regime dependence: on GSM8K ($\hat{\rho}=0.435$, $\hat{\theta}=+0.96$) the gap is dormant, whereas on AIME ($\hat{\rho}=0.480$, $\hat{\theta}=-0.15$) the single-unit rule crosses on 24% of prompts at nominal $\epsilon=0.05$, while the prompt-level procedure declines (0.00). This correctness result identifies which reliability claim an anytime-valid certificate actually supports under dependence.

cross PGX: Physically Grounded Execution for Long-Horizon Humanoid Loco-Manipulation

Authors: Kai Lu, Chenyang Ma, Ioannis Havoutis

Abstract: Robotic vision-language-action (VLA) models can execute diverse individual manipulation skills, yet they still struggle with long-horizon loco-manipulation tasks, where temporal structure, skills, and precision are tightly coupled within a monolithic model. We propose Physically Grounded Execution (PGX), a framework that offloads temporal and precision control to two classical training-free components, allowing the VLA to focus on stage-specific skill dynamics. PGX decomposes long-horizon loco-manipulation into three layers: (i) a predicate-based state machine governs task progression, using physical predicates to gate phase transitions, trigger recovery, and coordinate navigation and waiting. Shared predicate semantics connect offline demonstration segmentation, online execution, and stage-wise evaluation; (ii) VLA skills operate within active manipulation phases, with flat or stage-trained models concentrating on task-relevant actions; and (iii) world-coordinate locking (WCL) maintains spatial consistency through base station keeping, object-relative positioning, and world-anchored contact targets. We demonstrate the effectiveness of PGX with extensive experiments conducted on three simulated tasks and one real-world task using a Unitree G1 humanoid robot for long-horizon loco-manipulation, showing improvements in stage progression and task success rates. Project page is available at: https://kl-research.github.io/pgx

URLs: https://kl-research.github.io/pgx

cross Active Feature Acquisition by Distilling an Oracle Teacher

Authors: Yang Meng, Zixin Ding, Robert L. Grossman, Yuxin Chen

Abstract: Acquiring every feature of an instance is often costly: a measurement can carry a monetary price, a time delay, or a privacy cost. Active Feature Acquisition (AFA) addresses this by learning a policy that decides, per instance, which features to acquire under a budget. Existing AFA methods train this policy by optimizing a self-constructed utility target rather than distilling an explicit teacher. We construct this teacher explicitly. At training time the label and the unobserved feature values are available, and together they determine the teacher in closed form: an oracle that ranks every candidate by its effect on the downstream predictor's error. OracleImitator distills this ranking into a deployable policy by soft-target imitation. Across multimodal medical diagnosis and the AFABench benchmark, OracleImitator matches or outperforms existing AFA methods across the budget range, recovering 90% of the attainable accuracy gain at under a quarter of the acquisition cost. Because the teacher is an explicit algorithmic object rather than an implicit optimization target, we can also measure directly how much of its ranking is recoverable by a policy restricted to what is visible at deployment.

cross SoK: Assessing the Evolution of LLM Capability in Software Vulnerability Research

Authors: Amine Lbath, Manan Suri, Aurelien Delaitre, Vadim Okun, Massih-Reza Amini, Dinesh Manocha

Abstract: LLM-based agents are becoming increasingly capable at discovering, validating and patching vulnerabilities in real software, and vendors and researchers report that this is already changing how vulnerabilities are found and fixed. These claims, however, are scattered across papers, vendor reports and disclosures and are rarely comparable. We introduce a framework that rates these capabilities on an autonomy scale modeled on the levels of driving automation, and qualifies each claim by its correctness, its availability and the strength of its evidence. We use this framework to systematize the published evidence on LLM capability in vulnerability research. We find that agents discovered, validated, and patched vulnerabilities in real-world software without human intervention before the restricted frontier models of 2026, and that open-weight models subsequently attained comparable autonomy for discovery and validation. We then measure the consequences of evolving LLM security capabilities in a decade of public vulnerability records, advisories and exploitation data. In 2026 the number of disclosed vulnerabilities more than doubled, with an increasing share rated critical or high, and severe bugs that had survived a decade or more in widely used code were reported several times more often than before. Yet the time between the stages of a vulnerability's life, from report to fix and from disclosure to attack, has not shifted noticeably so far. We grade the mitigations proposed in the literature with an explicit rubric and draw guidance for each group of stakeholders from the best-supported ones.

cross One Prior, Two Dynamics: Repurposing Video Diffusion Transformers for Self-Supervised Latent Action Learning

Authors: Yuxin Jiang, Wenqiang Sun, Jiaqi Li, Yuchao Gu, Lvmin Zhang, Ivor Tsang, Mike Zheng Shou

Abstract: Videos reveal how the world changes, but rarely the actions that cause those changes. Latent action learning recovers such supervision from visual transitions, yet often learns the underlying dynamics largely from scratch. We ask whether a pretrained video generator can instead provide the prior for both inverse and forward dynamics. We introduce O$^{2}$LaF, built on a simple principle: one prior, two dynamics. We initialize both models from the same pretrained video DiT and adapt them to their distinct roles: a feed-forward transition encoder with learnable action queries for inverse dynamics, and a causal, action-conditioned video generator for forward dynamics. By controlling information access and optimizing both dynamics end-to-end through a compact bottleneck, O$^{2}$LaF learns transferable actions from unlabeled videos. Across navigation and robotic manipulation, O$^{2}$LaF sets a new state of the art in few-shot action probing and enables stronger zero-shot action transfer across visual contexts and embodiments. Beyond generative video priors, we further extend our framework to predictive representation priors such as V-JEPA2, achieving competitive performance. Our findings suggest a broader paradigm for latent-action learning: repurposing pretrained video models to discover transferable actions and predict their consequences.

replace LLM4Rail: An LLM-Augmented Personalized Railway Services Platform

Authors: Zhuo Li, Xianghuai Deng, Chiwei Feng, Hanmeng Li, Shenjie Wang, Junchao Fan, Jiaxing Shen, Zhiyuan Wen, Longxiang Gao, Linchun Wu

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have significantly reshaped different walks of business. To meet the increasing demands for individualized railway service, we develop LLM4Rail - a novel LLM-augmented personalized railway services platform. Empowered by the LLM, LLM4Rail provides dedicated modules for ticket and weather inquiries, food & drink recommendations, and casual conversation. In LLM4Rail, we propose the iterative "Experience-Retrieval Question-Thought-Action-Observation (ER-QTAO)" reasoning framework. It introduces two key mechanisms, Experience Storage and Retrieval (ESR) and Diagnostic Observation Generation (DOG), to enhance the LLM's ability to invoke appropriate external tools with complex arguments. replace AI GameStore: Scalable, Open-Ended Evaluation of Machine General Intelligence with Human Games

Authors: Lance Ying, Ryan Truong, Prafull Sharma, Kaiya Ivy Zhao, Nathan Cloos, Kelsey R. Allen, Thomas L. Griffiths, Katherine M. Collins, Jos\'e Hern\'andez-Orallo, Phillip Isola, Samuel J. Gershman, Joshua B. Tenenbaum

Abstract: Rigorously evaluating machine intelligence against the broad spectrum of human general intelligence has become increasingly important and challenging in this era of rapid technological advance. Conventional AI benchmarks typically assess only narrow capabilities in a limited range of human activity. Most are also static, quickly saturating as model capabilities improve. We propose that a more promising way to evaluate human-like general intelligence in AI systems is through a particularly strong form of general game playing: studying how and how well they play and learn to play all conceivable human games, in comparison to human players with the same level of experience, time, or other resources. We define a "human game" to be a game designed by humans for humans, and argue for the evaluative suitability of this space of all such games people can imagine and enjoy -- the "Multiverse of Human Games". Taking a first step towards this vision, we introduce the AI GameStore, a scalable and open-ended platform that uses LLMs with humans-in-the-loop to synthesize new representative human games, by automatically sourcing and adapting standardized and containerized variants of game environments from popular human digital gaming platforms. We generated 100 such games based on the top charts of Apple App Store and Steam, and evaluated seven frontier vision-language models (VLMs) on short episodes of play. The best models achieved less than 10% of the human average score on the majority of the games, and especially struggled with games that challenge world-model learning, memory and planning. We conclude with a set of next steps for building out the AI GameStore as a practical way to measure and drive progress toward human-like general intelligence in machines.

replace Agent^2 RL-Bench: Can LLM Agents Engineer Agentic RL Post-Training?

Authors: Wanyi Chen, Xiao Yang, Xu Yang, Tianming Sha, Qizheng Li, Zhuo Wang, Bowen Xian, Fang Kong, Weiqing Liu, Jiang Bian

Abstract: We introduce Agent^2 RL-Bench, a compact diagnostic benchmark for evaluating agentic RL post-training, which tests whether LLM agents can autonomously design, implement, debug, and execute post-training pipelines that improve foundation models. RL post-training increasingly drives model alignment and specialization, yet existing benchmarks are largely static, rewarding supervised fine-tuning or script generation without assessing an agent's ability to close an interactive RL loop. Agent^2 RL-Bench provides a unified agent-facing interface: each run starts from an isolated workspace containing a base model, task data, instructions, and a grading API, and agents must iterate within a fixed budget by training models and submitting artifacts for evaluation. The benchmark spans six tasks across three levels, from static rule-based training to judge-based optimization and closed-loop online RL with trajectory collection. Two diagnostic skills, namely runtime recording and post-hoc summarization, enable structured analysis of agent behavior, facilitating smooth and effective iteration of the benchmark's evaluation framework. Across five agent systems and six driver LLMs, agents show intelligent behavior but clear limitations: one RL-oriented run improves ALFWorld from 4.85 to 93.28 via SFT warm-up and GRPO with online rollouts, yet DeepSearchQA remains difficult, most successful routes rely on supervised pipelines, and interactive outcomes show large differences across agent stacks. Overall, Agent2 RL-Bench shows that current agents can sometimes engineer online RL, but stable agent-driven RL post-training remains rare under fixed budgets. It also demonstrates that our benchmark provides a strong and effective evaluation framework for future research in this direction. Code is available at https://github.com/microsoft/RD-Agent.

replace AI-Assisted Hypothesis Generation for Hospital Quality Improvement

Authors: Patrick Vossler, Jean Feng, Venkat Sivaraman, Robert Gallo, Hemal Kanzaria, Dana Freiser, Christopher Ross, Amy Ou, James Marks, Susan Ehrlich, Christopher Peabody, Lucas Zier

Abstract: Hospital quality improvement (QI) programs generally proceed in two stages: (1) hypothesis generation, which identifies candidate factors that may improve care quality and efficiency, and (2) confirmatory analyses, which implement and evaluate QI initiatives targeting these factors. Although most research focuses on the confirmatory stage, hypothesis generation is equally important because it shapes where teams focus their effort. Currently, hypothesis generation depends on expert-driven, qualitative methods (e.g., Lean Healthcare tools) that can be anecdotal, hard to reproduce, time-consuming, and resource-intensive. Large language models (LLMs) could accelerate this process by analyzing clinical notes at scale, but existing AI tools can only scale up well-defined tasks, whereas hypothesis generation is a "fuzzy," exploratory, and iterative process. We propose treating not only LLM instructions but also the problem specification and validation process as tunable natural-language (hyper)parameters. Through a procedure we term "Human-AI Spec-Solution Co-Optimization," we iteratively elicit clinical experts' definitions and judgments while aligning the AI pipeline with their intended criteria. We retrospectively applied this framework at an urban safety-net hospital to length of stay and 30-day unplanned readmissions. The final pipelines screen patient charts for potential factors and filter for clinically relevant ones by self-rating, and these self-ratings achieved over 70% concordance with human experts (within one Likert point) on held-out encounters. When scaled up hospital-wide, the pipelines identified a superset of the candidate factors found in prior, primarily manual analyses. By providing auditable reasoning traces and quantifying factor prevalence, the pipelines support a more quantitative and reproducible way to prioritize factors for prospective QI interventions.

replace Post-Reasoning: Improving the Performance of Non-Thinking Models at Negligible Cost

Authors: Richmond Sin Jing Xuan, Rishabh Bhardwaj, Soujanya Poria

Abstract: As the widespread adoption of Large Language Models (LLMs) accelerates, token consumption from intermediate reasoning traces increasingly contributes to inference latency and operational cost. Recent studies suggest that many real-world tasks require little to no explicit reasoning, with additional reasoning sometimes even degrading performance. In this work, we propose Post-Reasoning, a simple yet effective approach that improves instruction-tuned models by conditioning them to justify their answers after generating the final response. By design, it enables the final answer to be obtained with no reasoning tokens before the answer and with negligible latency and token cost, while still improving performance through simple instruction augmentation. We evaluate Post-Reasoning across 117 model benchmark settings spanning 13 open and proprietary models, 7 model families, and 9 diverse reasoning and knowledge-intensive benchmarks, including AMC, HMMT, GSM8K, GPQA, MMLU-Pro, and BIG-Bench Hard. Post-Reasoning improves performance in 84.62% of evaluated settings, achieving a mean relative improvement of 15.57%. Furthermore, we propose supervised post-reason tuning, which further improves performance in 91.11% of evaluated settings, and exceeds the prompt-based post-reasoning baseline by an average of 7.17%, demonstrating that post-reasoning can be effectively internalized through training. Ultimately, Post-Reasoning establishes a new performance ceiling for direct-answer capabilities.

replace Structured Role-Aware Policy Optimization for Multimodal Reasoning

Authors: Bingqing Jiang, Guoxi Zhang, Jasper Wang, Bingning Wang, Difan Zou

Abstract: Reinforcement learning from verifiable rewards (RLVR), especially with Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), has shown strong potential for improving the reasoning capabilities of large vision-language models (LVLMs). However, in multimodal reasoning, final-answer rewards are typically assigned at the sequence level and do not distinguish the functional roles of different tokens, making it difficult to determine whether a correct answer is supported by task-relevant visual evidence. In this paper, we revisit multimodal RLVR from the perspective of role-aware token-level credit assignment, where structured responses are decomposed into perception tokens for extracting visual evidence and reasoning tokens for deriving answers from that evidence. Based on this perspective, we propose Structured Role-aware Policy Optimization (SRPO), which refines the sequence-level GRPO advantage into role-aware token-level advantages without changing the reward function. Specifically, SRPO assigns role-specific credit by using self-distilled on-policy contrasts: perception tokens are emphasized according to their visual dependency under original versus corrupted visual inputs, while reasoning tokens are emphasized according to their consistency with the generated perception. These role-specific signals are further unified through a shared trajectory-level baseline, yielding positive token weights that adjust relative update magnitudes while preserving the original GRPO reward and advantage signs, without requiring external reward models or separate teachers. Experiments across diverse multimodal reasoning benchmarks show that SRPO improves evidence-grounded reasoning, highlighting the importance of moving beyond uniform sequence-level credit toward role-aware optimization for reliable multimodal reasoning.

replace Insights Generator: Systematic Corpus-Level Trace Diagnostics for LLM Agents

Authors: Akshay Manglik, Vijay S. Kalmath, Jason Qin, Apaar Shanker, Kaustubh Deshpande, Yash Maurya, Veronica Chatrath, Levi Lentz, Yuan Xue

Abstract: Diagnosing failures in LLM agents remains largely manual. Practitioners inspect a small subset of execution traces, form ad-hoc hypotheses, and iterate. This process misses patterns that only emerge across trace populations and does not scale to production corpora where individual traces span tens of thousands of tokens. We formalize the problem of corpus-level trace diagnostics. Given a corpus of execution traces, the goal is to produce grounded natural-language insights that characterize systematic behavioral patterns across trace groups, each linked to supporting evidence. We present the Insights Generator (IG), a multi-agent system that answers diagnostic questions by proposing and testing hypotheses across the trace corpus to produce an evidence-backed insights report. We evaluate IG across qualitative and objective dimensions, spanning rubric-based report assessment and downstream performance improvements achieved by implementing IG insights. Human experts using IG reports improve scaffold performance by 30.4 pp over the unmodified baseline scaffold, and coding agents leveraging IG-derived insights show consistent and stable gains. Across benchmarks, IG's scout-investigator architecture produces findings comparable in detection coverage to competing approaches, while domain experts rated IG reports as leading depth and evidence quality.

replace From "Weak" Signals to Strong Models: Preference Delta Aggregation with LoRA Merging

Authors: Qi Sun, Siyue Zhang, Yulin Chen, Yuxiang Xue, Ru Peng, Chen Zhao

Abstract: Training strong large language models (LLMs) requires high-quality supervision, which is often scarce. Recent work shows that paired preference data from weak-weaker model pairs (e.g., Qwen3 4B over 1.7B), despite the limited quality of individual responses, can provide an effective supervision signal through relative quality deltas, which we term a "weak" signal. This motivates a key research question: can multiple "weak" signals be constructively aggregated for improving strong models (e.g., Qwen3 8B)? To this end, we propose Preference Delta Aggregation (PDA), the first framework that derives a preference delta from each weak-weaker model pair, instantiates it as a LoRA adapter learned through preference optimization, and aggregates the resulting deltas via LoRA merging. To improve the composition of independently trained adapters, we introduce Geometric Alignment Merging (GAM), which aligns their left and right singular bases through orthogonal Procrustes rotations and averages the aligned factors. Evaluations on knowledge reasoning and agentic search show that PDA-GAM exceeds the strongest individual delta, with further gains along the evaluated sequences of additional deltas. Using three reasoning deltas and four agentic deltas, PDA-GAM improves Qwen3-8B by 6.8 and 7.3 points on average, respectively, exceeding the strongest single-delta baseline by 2.1 and 4.3 points. These configurations achieve the highest average scores among the evaluated merging methods, with similar gains observed on a second student model. Further analysis suggests that these gains come from composing complementary capabilities encoded across distinct preference deltas. Code is available at https://github.com/AlbertQiSun/Preference-Delta-Aggregation.

URLs: https://github.com/AlbertQiSun/Preference-Delta-Aggregation.

replace VESTA: Visual Exploration with Statistical Tool Agents

Authors: William Rudman, Abhishek Divekar, Kanishk Jain, Sebastian Joseph, Stella S. R. Offner, Matthew Lease, Kyle Mahowald, Greg Durrett, Junyi Jessy Li

Abstract: Fitting quantitative models to data is a central step in scientific workflows, yet it remains one of the least automated. Recent agent-based systems leverage language and vision-language models (VLMs) to propose statistical models, but these systems struggle on challenging modeling tasks. To address these limitations, we introduce VESTA (Visual Exploration with Statistical Tool Agents), a framework that equips VLMs with a dynamically growing toolkit to guide model refinement through data transformations, hypothesis-driven visualizations, and statistical tests. Unlike prior systems that rely on iterative critique alone, VESTA explores data before and during refinement by selecting or creating reusable diagnostic tools. We evaluate VESTA using three toolkit configurations: no tools, static expert-written tools, and dynamic model-written tools. To support this evaluation, we introduce DAWN (Dataset for Automated Workflows and Numerical Modeling), a benchmark targeting distribution fitting and time series modeling with varying difficulty tiers that culminates in real-world astronomy tasks including modeling initial mass functions and gravitational-wave chirp signals. VESTA's dynamic tool creation outperforms prior agentic pipelines for statistical analysis and visual tool creation, with the largest gains on complex and domain-specific tasks. Against strong agentic coding harnesses (Claude Code, Codex, and OpenCode), VESTA is better on all time series tasks and on complex distribution fitting tasks, though the harnesses achieve better fits on simpler distributions. replace Business World Model

Authors: Cecil Pang, Hiroki Sayama

Abstract: World model has emerged as a powerful paradigm in artificial intelligence, enabling agents to represent their environments, predict future states, and evaluate possible actions before acting. However, existing world models have largely been developed for visual or physical domains such as computer vision, robotics, gaming, and autonomous driving, which are governed by relatively stable dynamics. These formulations are not directly applicable to business practice, where the relevant environment is semantic, organizational, and market driven. Outcomes depend on context-sensitive factors such as customer behavior, pricing, competition, and regulation. This paper introduces the concept and architecture of a Business World Model, a world model specialized for business and organizational environments. We propose a business-semantics-centric formulation in which states, dynamics, and feasible actions are linked to business entities, their attributes, and their relationships. Within this framework, intelligent agents can simulate alternative action sequences, estimate their effects on future business outcomes, and evaluate trade-offs under uncertainty and business constraints. We present a reference design and open-source implementation in which LLM agents interpret natural-language business goals and explain results, while a deterministic, metadata-driven simulation engine integrating machine learning models, business rules, and action spaces performs all calculations. A churn case study of a digital subscription business demonstrates that the system recommends options meeting the churn-reduction target within the revenue constraint. This work establishes a conceptual foundation for autonomous business systems capable of moving from instruction-based execution toward goal-driven planning, optimization, and execution.

replace Multi-Objective Structured Pruning of LLMs for Latency and Model Size Optimization

Authors: Muhammad Junaid Ali, Smail Niar, El-Ghazali Talbi

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) have achieved widespread adoption because of their strong reasoning and query-response capabilities. However, deploying them in embedded and edge computing environments remains challenging because of strict latency, memory, and energy constraints. Their large parameter counts and computational demands hinder efficient execution on resource-constrained platforms. Although model pruning has emerged as a viable solution for reducing scale while preserving performance, jointly optimizing layers, attention heads, and Multi-Layer Perceptron (MLP) dimensions remains highly complex. Exhaustively exploring this combined design space is computationally expensive and often leads to local optima or unstable configurations. To address these limitations, we propose a hardware-aware, multi-objective structured pruning framework. The proposed two-stage method explicitly targets latency and model size for efficient deployment on edge devices. In the coarse-grained stage, multi-objective depth pruning removes entire attention and MLP blocks to reduce computational load and memory usage. In the subsequent fine-grained stage, Parallel Bayesian Optimization (PBO) searches for the optimal layer-wise pruning ratios for pruning under latency constraints, while importance-based strategies rank the specific components to be pruned within each layer's allocated budget. Experimental results show that our approach reduces model complexity with minimal impact on commonsense reasoning tasks and zero-shot performance. Our method achieves a favorable trade-off among accuracy, latency, and model size, making it suitable for edge deployment. Across multiple LLMs at 37.5% and 50% pruning ratios, the proposed approach achieves better performance on commonsense reasoning tasks than existing methods while significantly reducing inference cost.

replace Tokengeist: Multi-Turn Attribution Tracing in Agentic Conversations

Authors: Jessica Tang, Shraddha Barke, Sharad Agarwal

Abstract: When a model answers after several turns of dialogue and tool use, an overseer needs to know which earlier turns and intermediate steps it rests on. Attribution is the natural primitive for answering this, but existing context attribution methods process the full transcript in a single pass: they recover surface-level dependencies while missing the layered structure of multi-turn traces, in which an assistant turn is itself a derived artifact that condenses or transforms earlier context. We introduce multi-turn context attribution (MTCA): given a target span in a model response, the task of tracing attribution backward across turns to identify not only which prior turns were directly relevant, but also how those turns themselves depended on earlier context. We propose Tokengeist, an attribution-method-agnostic and scalable framework that recovers full dependency paths by casting attribution as a recursive traversal of a directed acyclic graph over conversation turns. We will release MTCABench, a benchmark of 3,970 target spans across 685 multi-turn conversations, annotated with gold provenance graphs reaching depths of up to 17, across four dependency types. Across four open-weight models, flat attribution methods fail to recover multi-hop dependencies, with source recall improving from 11-38% under flat attribution to 82-94% under Tokengeist. Our results reveal a systematic failure mode of single-pass attribution, which we term provenance collapse, where attribution terminates at the nearest assistant restatement instead of the originating event.

replace Cautious Context Steering for Language Model Personalization

Authors: Gihoon Kim, Jeyoung Lee, Suhan Woo, Jangwon Oh, Sekwon Oh, Minsu Jeon, Euntai Kim

Abstract: User context can personalize a language model without per-user fine-tuning, but its influence is not equally helpful at every token. Context Steering (CoS) uses a fixed steering strength throughout generation and requires two LM forward passes per step. We propose Cautious Context Steering (CCS), a lightweight adapter that learns when and how strongly to steer a frozen LM using user context. We train the adapter through cautious distillation, using oracle targets built from predictions with and without context. It learns to match the steered distribution where context helps predict the user's preferred token and the context-free base distribution elsewhere. The CCS adapter outperforms both training-free context-based methods and trained personalization baselines in preference alignment across benchmarks. We also introduce GSM8K-P to evaluate task accuracy and task-specific format preferences together. CCS achieves high format adherence while maintaining task accuracy, offering a better balance than the baselines. The CCS adapter adds less than 0.4% of the backbone's parameters. At inference, each decoding step uses one context-conditioned LM forward pass and a lightweight adapter evaluation, reducing generation time on PRISM by approximately 35-36% compared with CoS.

replace CHORUS: Coverage-Guided Agentic RL and Policy Consolidation for Testbench Stimulus Generation

Authors: Hejia Zhang, Sheng Lu, Zhijing Wu, Yitong Ma, Zhongming Yu, Wentao Ni, Chia-Tung Ho, Jishen Zhao

Abstract: Testbenches written by large language models can compile and run while leaving entire datapaths idle. We present CHORUS, the first framework to combine coverage-guided agentic reinforcement learning (RL) with policy consolidation for testbench stimulus generation. We train LLM4Cov's final supervised checkpoint to draft testbenches and repair its lowest-scoring draft using simulator feedback, raising CVDP-ECov agentic Pass@1 from 69.2% to 85.5%. replace Fool's Gold: Defensive Deception Against Safety-Removal Attacks on Open-Weight Models

Authors: Mark Russinovich

Abstract: Safety alignment in open-weight language models is trivially removable: abliteration projects a refusal-mediating direction out of the weights in minutes, and no release-time defense we are aware of prevents it durably. What cannot be prevented can be deceived. Our defense, decoy hardening ("Fool's Gold"), concedes the refusal strip and poisons its payoff: once refusal is stripped, most answers to hazardous operational requests are confident, fluent decoys whose critical elements are falsified. Decoys are trained inside a differentiable simulation of the attack, expressing only in the attacked state; a refusal pin and benign leash hold clean-state behavior to the original. We instantiate it on seven models from five families (9B-122B, dense and mixture-of-experts). On the six models passing our pre-registered efficacy gate, 0.51-0.90 of attacked-state responses to held-out prompts are decoys, +0.27-0.84 attributable to the defense; all six stay within registered benign-behavior and capability budgets; the seventh (smaller) fails the gate (boundary case). Rates replicate on a frozen test split or untouched strata. The claim is epistemic: without independent ground truth, no observation surface we tested separates falsified answers from correct ones - on external red-team benchmarks' CBRNE-adjacent slice, the defended 122B is fatally wrong on 0.82-0.86 of matched-quality answers vs at most 0.10 undefended. Repeated sampling does not restore trust: element-wise consensus at K=64 reconstructs a fully usable procedure on 0.083-0.625 of prompts where the instrument validates, vs 0.58-0.96 undefended, with no label-free way to tell the regimes apart; on the weakest such model the claim is per-draw only. We evaluate chemical and biological hazards; the defense does not address in-context jailbreaks and protects only the initially released defended weights.

replace Measurement-Budget Allocation in Quantum Learning with Finite-Shot Generalization Guarantees

Authors: Ferhat Ozgur Catak

Abstract: On near-term quantum hardware, estimating a Born probability requires repeated circuit executions. A quantum learning experiment with a fixed measurement budget $B$ must therefore decide how many distinct training states $n$ to use and how many shots $S$ to spend on each. We study this trade-off for binary quantum classifiers whose measurement operator $M$ is learned from the training data and evaluated with fresh measurement outcomes. We prove a distribution-free generalization bound for bounded, $L$-Lipschitz losses with a sample term of order $\sqrt{d/n}$ and a shot term of order $\sqrt{(\log n)/S}$; under $B=nS$ the two terms move in opposite directions. Minimizing a closed-form surrogate of the bound gives the allocation rule $n^* = 2\sqrt{2dB/\log(4B/\delta)}$, $S^* = B/n^*$, whose bound value is within 0.1% of the exact numerical minimum on all budgets we evaluate, and a worst-case rate of $B^{-1/4}$. PennyLane simulations with 2- and 4-qubit variational circuits on nine synthetic benchmarks show that the empirical gap stays below the bound in all configurations, although the bound is vacuous at the simulated budgets and becomes non-vacuous only for larger budgets (e.g., $B \gtrsim 5000$ for $d=4$). For a loss that is concave in the score, the empirical gap is U-shaped under a fixed budget and the measured shot contribution matches its analytical value; for the absolute loss, shot noise is unbiased and no shot-limited regime appears. The rule can be used to plan finite-shot evaluations before an experiment is run.

replace Balance of Benchmarks: Evidence Retention and Multiplicity-Aware Model Comparison

Authors: Jhen-Ke Lin, Hong-Yun Lin

Abstract: Benchmark aggregation requires decisions about how much evidence to use and how much influence each benchmark receives. Curated suites control influence through selection, which can exclude informative observations. A broader pool can retain those observations. With equal weighting, repeated or closely related benchmarks gain influence as their number grows. We introduce Balance of Benchmarks (BoB) to retain eligible observations and control their influence separately. We fit benchmark-specific response curves to place different difficulty ranges on a shared ability scale and use description similarity to downweight benchmarks in crowded neighborhoods. We test retention and density weighting in separate interventions on WildScores, a sparse collection of 148 developer-reported benchmarks, using 14 Artificial Analysis benchmarks as a controlled reference. With the model population fixed and entire source-lineage families held out, suite expansion improves held-out prediction under BoB and equal-weight equating on both WildScores and BIG-bench. Under substantial reporting overlap, density weighting improves robustness to multiplicity. Complete re-listings yield smaller mean rank displacement under BoB (0.63 versus 1.95 for equal-weight equating), with benefits also in paraphrased re-listings and supported affine fits. The benefit can reverse if models report different benchmarks, because a single weight per column gives models that report only part of a group less than its intended share. We therefore compute each model's weights from the benchmarks it reports. This rule removes the reversal in controlled re-listings and keeps held-out prediction close to that of equal weighting. These comparisons separate evidence retention, benchmark influence, and reporting coverage and show why repeated entries need to be handled per model.

replace EvoSkill Injection: Red-Teaming Autonomous Skill Generation and Evolution in Self-Evolving Agents

Authors: Doyun Kim, Chanwoo Kim, Sugyeong Eo, Yeo-Chan Yoon, Chanjun Park

Abstract: LLM-based agent systems increasingly adopt skill-based architectures to reduce repetitive reasoning costs and improve stable, efficient task execution. Recent studies propose self-evolving agents that autonomously generate, refine, and reuse skills from past experiences to enable continuous capability evolution. However, autonomous skill evolution introduces a new attack surface in which malicious capabilities are generated, stored, and reused as legitimate skills. In this paper, we define EvoSkill Injection as a threat model targeting the autonomous skill generation and evolution pipeline of self-evolving agents. We further propose SARGE (Red-teaming Autonomous Skill Generation and Evolution in self-evolving agents), a red-teaming framework for evaluating this threat model through iterative generation, escalation, and reinforcement interactions. To support our framework, we construct EvoSkillBench, a benchmark dataset of malicious interaction trajectories for inducing malicious skill formation in self-evolving agents, and introduce EvoSkillSafetyBench, a post-attack benchmark for evaluating whether injected malicious skills are subsequently retrieved and activated as harmful behaviors. Our evaluation shows that SARGE induces malicious skill formation and that injected skills are persistently stored and repeatedly activated, highlighting the risk of persistent capability corruption.

replace Homeostatic Continual Learning

Authors: Yue Jin

Abstract: I formulate a Continual Learning problem for an autonomous agent that imitates the one animals face. The agent has intents to satisfy. It experiences contexts where different combinations of features are active and impact intent outcomes. I propose a method named ``Homeostatic Continual Learning'' that imitates the one animals use. The agent learns incrementally environment data and features relevant for their intents from the impact of contexts, gradually completes its model and policy and performs well in more and more contexts. The core of the method is to find outliers in the environment data when the agent experiences an outlier in its intent outcome after the context changes. This method enables the agent to learn continuously in changing contexts without catastrophic forgetting. When pursuing many intents concurrently, the agent may build a transformer-like world model with the method where it factorizes the objects in the world into features, abstract objects into comparable instances of concepts and map concepts to intents through features. The world model is composed of semantic nodes and explainable by nature. I discuss the works needed to render the method practical, the connections to many fields in Artificial Intelligence and the broader implications of the method.

replace LimiX-2: A Contextual Mechanism Network Towards General Structured-Data Intelligence

Authors: Xingxuan Zhang, Gang Ren, Hao Yuan, Hao Zou, Hongze Tan, Hui Wang, Jianhao Song, Jiansheng Li, Jiayao Zhang, Jinghan Zhang, Kaifang Li, Lang Mo, Li Mao, Mingchao Hao, Nuo Xu, Rui Ding, Ruiji Zhang, Shuyang Li, Siyu Mei, Tianyang Zhang, Weiyang Mu, Yancheng Dong, Yongxian Wei, Yuan Xue, Yuanrui Wang, Yue He, Zijia Yang, Ziyun Li, Dongzhe Li, Fuqiang Wang, Jiandong Liu, Jiawei Chen, Jiaxin Du, Kaijie Cheng, Kehan Li, Lei Sun, Linjun Zhou, Ningbo Dai, Qi Wang, Renzhe Xu, Shaoxing Du, Shumeng Yang, Wang Lu, Wenjing Chu, Xiannan Huang, Xiaoyu Lin, Xing Ai, Xinyan Han, Xuanyue Li, Xuanyue Su, Xukun Zhang, Yan Lu, Yaxin Zhang, Yi Qin, Yifei Huang, Yihan Xu, Yongle Lv, Yuanyuan Jiang, Yushan Han, Peng Cui

Abstract: We introduce LimiX-2, a new model in the LimiX family, developed through model and data scaling guided by our previously established scaling laws. LimiX-2 adopts the Contextual Mechanism Networks (CMNs) paradigm and is pretrained with Context-Conditional Masked Modeling (CCMM). CMNs shifts the organizing principle of in-context learning from target-centric prediction to mechanism-oriented joint modeling. Rather than centering the network on the $p(y \mid x, D_{\mathrm{context}})$ objective of conventional tabular PFNs, it is designed around learning $p(x, y \mid D_{\mathrm{context}})$, a context-dependent representation of the joint structure underlying data generation. Pretraining uses synthetic datasets generated by structural causal models (SCMs) spanning diverse graph structures, functional mechanisms, and observation processes. Evaluations on TabArena, TALENT, and BCCO show that LimiX-2 outperforms current dataset-specific models and tabular foundation models. Beyond predictive performance, the CMN paradigm also promotes causal awareness in LimiX-2: its feature attention encodes direct causal relationships, enabling accurate causal skeleton recovery.

replace QVAC Genesis III: A Large-Scale, High-Quality Open Synthetic STEM Corpus for Efficient Language Model Pre-Training

Authors: Davide Vitabile, N. Ranjan, Akshay Nambiar, Kamal K. Gupta, Amril Nazir

Abstract: High-quality pre-training data is a critical bottleneck for educational and STEM-specific language models targeting edge AI and on-device deployment where token budgets are tightly constrained. While major organizations train ever-larger models on private corpora, the open ecosystem lacks STEM-focused synthetic datasets that deliver high per-token learning value efficiently for small models. To address this gap, we introduce QVAC Genesis III, a 191.43B-token, STEM-focused multi-domain synthetic corpus covering 19 domains across several difficulty levels and different educational styles. QVAC Genesis III is built via a dual generation strategy that performs targeted teacher distillation using a weak edge-scale student model as signal: the student's failures are converted into corrective explanations, while its successes are expanded into contrastive option-level reasoning over all answer choices. We further introduce an LLM-as-a-parser evaluation protocol that extracts final answers from free-form outputs and tracks both accuracy and answer validity. To validate the effectiveness of our QVAC Genesis III data, we conduct controlled from-scratch ablations with 1.7B-parameter models, showing that models trained with QVAC Genesis III consistently outperform both models trained with the open-source synthetic corpus Cosmopedia-v2 and the publicly released Cosmo-1B model across ARC, GPQA Diamond, and MMLU STEM benchmarks, achieving up to +28.57% on ARC-E and +21.35% on ARC-C, while reaching a Valid Answer Rate of up to 99.45%.

replace Designer-RSI: Evolving Procedural Memory for Graphic Design Agent

Authors: Hongyang Du, Lan Yan, Christian Flores, Asim Kadav

Abstract: Deployed software agents face a continuous stream of user requests while their foundation-model weights remain frozen between releases. Recursive self-improvement (RSI) via external memory offers a practical path to continual adaptation, yet prior methods largely assume programmatic verifiers, cheap retries, or a single global prompt. Creative software breaks these assumptions, as open-ended visual briefs lack ground truth, VLM judges are noisy, and multi-step tool executions are costly and confounded by external assets. We present Designer-RSI, which evolves a modular SKILL.md skill bank from unlabeled design trajectories for an agent spanning over 230 raster, vector, and layout tools. Designer-RSI couples widening (acquiring new skills for recurring capability gaps) with deepening (revising failing skills by contrasting successful and failed runs), and screens updates through a Pareto-style matched replay gate that requires strict gains on sampled briefs without local regressions. Across held-out product briefs, eight external benchmarks, and two backbones, traffic-driven evolution outperforms static documentation skills, lifts execution success up to 99.8%, improves requirement fulfillment, and reaches up to a 67.6% blind win rate over the base agent. Ablations further show that skill acquisition and revision are strongly synergistic: neither suffices alone, and reliable gains require evolving coverage and execution quality together.

replace Harness-Zero: Harness Distillation via Agent-as-Harness

Authors: Haoran Ye, Yuxing Lu, Haonan Dong, Zhaochen Su, Guojie Song

Abstract: Agent harnesses, the external systems that mediate model-environment interaction, can substantially improve agent performance, but their gains remain tied to the harness at deployment. Because the best harness varies across domains, instances, and models, a general-purpose agent must either settle for a suboptimal shared harness or route among an ever-growing set of specialized ones. We therefore study agent harness distillation: using a domain- or instance-optimized harness as training-time guidance and transferring the behaviors it induces into model weights, so that its gains survive under a single fixed target harness. The challenge is that the two harnesses differ in action space and available information, so guidance from the optimized harness cannot serve directly as supervision for the target one. We introduce Harness-Zero, which enables harness distillation through agent-as-harness. Guided by the optimized harness, a harnessing agent corrects student responses before execution in the target harness's action space, turning harness guidance into training demonstrations. Fine-tuning on the resulting trajectories internalizes harness-induced behavior into the model, so the specialized harness can be removed at deployment. Our experiments spanning knowledge work, tool use, and science domains show that: (1) For frontier LLMs using the same evolved harness, agent-as-harness outperforms code-as-harness. (2) With the specialized harness removed at deployment, Harness-Zero improves the base model's macro-average task success from 23.3% to 44.3%, even exceeding the 41.7% it reaches with that harness still attached, while reducing inference token usage by 27%-86%. (3) Harness-Zero recovers harness-induced behaviors absent from the base model, with 82.3% average recovery across 28 patterns in the three domains.

replace Evaluating Real-Time Voice Agents: A Survey from Component Quality to Grounded Outcomes

Authors: Shivam Negi, Arpit Rawat, Rashi Jain

Abstract: Real-time voice agents must decide when to speak, recover from disruption, and act only when the conversation requires it. But currently their evaluation is fragmented and is split across speech quality, turn-taking, tool calling, and task completion. We review 136 primary works published up to October 2, 2026, and code each on two axes: the capability an evaluation exercises, namely timing, recovery, grounded outcome, and, where relevant, multiparty conduct; and the strength of its evidence, namely evaluation conditions and grading instrument. The coding locates the gaps. Only 13 of 91 evaluation works check external state or action traces, only 5 of 21 grounded-outcome benchmarks use real human speech, and only 2 of 26 multiparty benchmarks verify state. Three claims follow. Architecture constrains deployment but does not determine results. It has been observed that benchmarks can verify outcomes, but few do. Evaluating an agent solely through one-on-one user interaction leaves critical gaps: it cannot confirm if the agent is speaking to the correct person, verify that person's authority, or identify the proper moments for strategic silence. From the taxonomy we derive TRG, short for Timing-Recovery-Grounded, a minimum reporting standard, and apply it to three published reports with different architectures. In one, tool-selection F1 is 82.5% while exact-call success is 33.0%, with no evidence of external task state. None of the three reports measures task state after an interruption. TRG governs what is reported and assigns no composite score. Per-source codes and tooling accompany the submission.

replace DeepEdu-F: A Framework for Efficient and Scalable Agentic LLMs in Vietnamese Education

Authors: Quang Nguyen, Hieu Nguyen, Toan Pham, Hien Hoang, Cong Tran, Nam Vu

Abstract: Cloud AI assistants send student data to foreign servers, conflicting with data-sovereignty laws such as Vietnam's Decree 53, and are not grounded in the national curriculum, so they often hallucinate on local content. Self-hosting an open model keeps data on-premise but faces two costs that weight quantization does not remove. An AI tutoring session revisits the same long material every turn, so once prefix caching removes the prefill cost, decoding over a growing KV Cache dominates response time; and grounding the tutor in local knowledge by fine-tuning is too costly to repeat as that knowledge changes. We present DeepEdu-F, a framework for the Vietnamese AI tutor DeepEdu with two components. LastSet selects the critical KV Cache entries once per turn from the question's attention, decodes the whole turn from that set, and keeps the full cache so later questions can select again. REPAIR (Reliable Evidence-based Playbook Adaptation and Insight Revision) curates a verified playbook from past interactions instead of updating weights. LastSet decodes up to 3.1x faster than dense decoding at a 256k-token context, faster than four sparse baselines, with accuracy close to full attention. Compared with ACE (ADD), REPAIR raises final accuracy from 70.0% to 80.5% on Formula and from 53.6% to 59.7% on FiNER, while mean task/scenario goal completion across AppWorld's normal and challenge splits increases from 9.28% to 16.28%.

replace One Latent, Many Tokens: Jointly Learning Compressed Embeddings for Efficient Language Diffusion

Authors: Yulin Yuan, Ying Zhang, Xiangming Meng

Abstract: Most continuous diffusion language models process one latent position per token at each sampling step, making generation expensive. Two-stage methods lower the cost by reducing the latent length, but they fix the compressed embedding space before training the diffusion model. Embeddings from the fixed space can be difficult to model with diffusion and decode reliably into tokens, which limits generation quality after compression. To address this problem, we introduce JPEG-DLM (Joint-embedding Prediction for Efficient Generation with Diffusion Language Model), which jointly trains a compressor, a flow matching model and a decoding module. With joint-embedding prediction, JPEG-DLM learns compressed embeddings that are more structured, easier to model with diffusion and reliably decodable into tokens.JPEG-DLM achieves the highest throughput among recent diffusion and flow models on LM1B and OWT, with a Gen-PPL lower than most of them. At a compression rate of 0.5 on OWT, it reaches a Gen-PPL of 34.52 and approximately 2.3 times ELF's throughput. These results suggest that jointly learning compressed embeddings offers a promising path toward efficient diffusion language modeling.Code is available at https://github.com/infiniteYuanyl/JPEG-DLM.

URLs: https://github.com/infiniteYuanyl/JPEG-DLM.

replace A General Harness for Protein Foundation Model Fitness Prediction

Authors: Yang Tan, Qijia Tian, Gangyu Sun, Bozitao Zhong, Mingchen Li, Yuanxi Yu, Nanqing Dong, Liang Hong

Abstract: Accurate fitness prediction is central to protein engineering and understanding sequence-function relationships. With advances in deep learning, protein foundation models (PFMs) have become widely used for this task. Recent analyses, however, show that these models share preferences reflecting their training corpora, while unreliable inputs can further distort fitness predictions. Family-specific evolutionary evidence and structural context can help address these limitations by providing complementary constraints on model scores, motivating VenusREM-Harness (VRH), a general, model-agnostic, training-free Retrieval-Enhanced Mutation harness. Built with VRH, VenusREM2 is the first to rank highest in all function, taxon, MSA-depth, and mutation-depth categories, with a ProteinGym Average Spearman of 0.556, 0.038 above the prior best.

replace UpliftMem: Learning Set-Level Uplift for Agent Memory Retrieval

Authors: Mengkun Liang, Haoran Qiang, Guannan Liu, Junjie Wu

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents draw on external memory to guide new tasks, raising the question of which memories to retrieve together to improve execution. Learning set values requires outcome feedback, but evaluating each untested alternative costs an additional rollout. We introduce \textsc{UpliftMem}, which learns set-level execution uplift relative to the same executor without memory. Our analysis of how retrieval preferences limit feedback coverage motivates targeted probes. Under a correlated Gaussian surrogate, we derive a closed-form expected value of sample information (EVSI) criterion that prioritizes probes by their expected benefit to local retrieval decisions. The feedback trains a shared scorer with the executor frozen, and inference requires no probing. Across ALFWorld, WebShop, and BigCodeBench, \textsc{UpliftMem} achieves best or joint-best success rates among evaluated methods. On fixed-store MemSyco-Bench evaluations, decision accuracy increases from 41.7\% to 66.5\% with Qwen3-4B and from 44.4\% to 68.8\% with Qwen3-8B. At matched probe budgets, EVSI improves decision accuracy over random probing by 10.6 and 13.9 percentage points, respectively.

replace Can Computation from Earlier Problems Help LLMs Solve New Ones?

Authors: Jipei He, Wenhui Tan, Xiaoyi Yu, Enver Sangineto, Fiorenzo Parascandolo, Rita Cucchiara, Ruihua Song

Abstract: Large language models often solve independent problems in the same conversation. Can computation from earlier problems help them solve new ones? Preliminary experiments show that retained history can raise or lower later-turn accuracy, even within the same domain. To trace this effect, we hold each problem fixed and vary its preceding history. The resulting changes in the model's internal states contain a recurring, problem-dependent component. To improve reasoning with retained history, we introduce STAIR (Stale-Token Attention for Inter-query Reuse). It captures attention keys and values from earlier response generation in a read-only bank. Across three Qwen models and four reasoning benchmarks, STAIR improves average later-turn accuracy by up to 11.67 percentage points over the unmodified model with history. replace Releasing the Inner Jev: A Simple Recipe for Open Decision Models

Authors: Jonathan Geuter, Andrew Cheng, David Alvarez-Melis

Abstract: LLM-based decision-making systems, also called System One models, have recently gained popularity, most notably through Jev (Almeida, 2026). Given a typed input state, a question, and a set of options, they return a calibrated probability for each option in a single forward pass, without text generation. After fine-tuning, it scores 89.0, outperforming Jev by 8.9 Capability points and on par with the strongest open decision model we evaluated. The same recipe improves the three other models by 7.8 to 15.7 points. On a suite of public decision datasets, our models achieve accuracy and calibration comparable to Jev. Our results show that open LLMs already possess System One capabilities, and with simple fine-tuning they outperform Jev on a wide range of decision tasks.
