replace ResearchFlow-Eval: Tracing the Execution Gap in End-to-End Multi-Claim Autoresearch

Authors: Peng Jiang, Dongyang Li, Quanying Liu, Xiaoxuan Jia

Abstract: Autoresearch systems increasingly undertake entire research projects. In practice, scientific research requires systems to pursue multiple interdependent claims through a continuous, multi-stage workflow and revise experiments as evidence accumulates. Existing evaluations of autoresearch systems, however, typically score isolated tasks or final artifacts, leaving these process-level capabilities largely unmeasured. We therefore introduce ResearchFlow-Eval, a fine-grained framework for assessing continuous scientific execution and iteration at both the stage and claim levels. Each evaluation is grounded in a published anchor paper, from which we derive stage-specific rubrics that decompose each research stage into dimensions and separately scored rubric items. Using these rubrics, we score each research stage and examine experimental execution in detail, focusing on multi-claim completion and revisions across attempts. We apply the evaluation framework to general-purpose agent harnesses, skill-augmented harnesses, and systems with research-specific orchestration. Averaged over two runs per system, each scored by two LLM judges, experimental execution lags behind design and planning for every evaluated system. Within execution, systems struggle to produce high-quality results and meet claim requirements, and repeated attempts rarely yield measurable improvements. Moreover, neither skill augmentation nor research-specific orchestration reliably yields higher scores. ResearchFlow-Eval thus extends autoresearch evaluation beyond isolated, single-objective runs to continuous workflows in which systems pursue multiple claims and refine their work across iterations.

replace What the Trace Shows and What the Label Says: Auditing Behavioral Detectors in Agent Evaluation

Authors: Idunnuoluwa A. Adeniji, Shilpika, Michael E. Papka

Abstract: LLM agents are often evaluated by reducing execution traces to labels such as final-answer correctness, tool verification, and fault propagation. These labels are measurements produced by parsers, behavioral detectors, and harness rules, yet evaluations rarely report whether a measurement actually fits the trace it describes. We study what happens when those measurements do not fully capture the trace's behavior. Agents solve GSM8K problems with a calculator whose outputs are occasionally corrupted, which gives ground truth for what the tool returned, what the model later used, and what the detectors reported; a chemistry task with search and retrieval tests the case where the model cannot recompute the tool's output. Across eight models and up to 1,000 traces per model, final correctness, visible re-verification, and fault propagation disagree in systematic ways. The most common gap is the silent tool-output override: the model replaces a corrupted value without calling the tool again, so the error stops propagating while a verification detector records no re-check. Human review finds verification labels the least reliable (\k{appa} =0.29), and checking depends on the task: the same calculator that several models re-checked on arithmetic was never re-checked on chemistry. Execution limits and serving failures also change what is measured. These findings motivate measurement transparency: an automated label should be reported with its evidence, whether the detector's question applied, whether the run was censored, and any human corrections. Our trace viewer makes this information inspectable alongside the reported results.

replace Noise Steering: Exploiting Wake Steering and Acoustic Directivity for Noise-Constrained Wind Farm Control via Reinforcement Learning

Authors: Mart\'in de Frutos, Baptiste Corban, Claire Bizon Monroc, Ana Bu\v{s}i\'c, Donatien Dubuc, Jiamin Zhu, Esteban Ferrer

Abstract: Existing noise mitigation strategies for wind farms usually consist in reducing turbines power output, without accounting for the wake interactions between them and the noise emission directivity. This leads to over-conservative policies and unexploited wind power. Yet, turbine yaw can simultaneously affect wake interactions and the direction of noise emission, offering an additional degree of freedom for farm-level noise control. We therefore propose Noise Steering, a model-free reinforcement learning (RL) strategy that jointly controls turbine yaw angles and power setpoints to maximise farm power while satisfying a local noise constraint in a defined region. To enable training at a reduced computational cost, a low-fidelity acoustic model is coupled with the steady-state FLORIS simulator. The RL agent effectively learns how to redirect wakes and noise directivity away from the protected region while redistributing power across the farm. On a six-turbine test farm, the policy satisfies the 40 dB noise constraint throughout the full range of turbulent winds, with an average power deficit of only 2% relative to greedy unimpeded control, and up to 7.7% at high wind speeds. In comparison, the industry-standard noise-reduced operation (NRO) baseline here generates an average power loss of 17%. When deployed in the higher-fidelity FASTFarm simulator, the policy largely retains its performance under steady and turbulent inflow, despite differences in simulation fidelity. These results demonstrate the potential of combining yaw control and power derating through RL to improve the power-noise trade-off in wind farm operation.

replace Kairos: A Unified PDDL Planner for Robots: Plans in Milliseconds, with a Guarantee for Every Answer

Authors: Francisco Mart\'in Rico, Esther Aguado Gonz\'alez, Francisco Miguel Moreno Olivo, Rodrigo P\'erez Rodr\'iguez, Jos\'e Miguel Guerrero Hern\'andez, Francisco J. Rodr\'iguez Lera

Abstract: Robots that plan while they act need a task planner that reads the models their designers write, answers within milliseconds, and says what each answer means. Models are written in the Planning Domain Definition Language (PDDL), which has grown from classical planning to durative actions, numeric resources, time windows and continuous change, while planners cover one fragment each and differ in what their failures mean. The contribution in artificial intelligence is Kairos, a single planner, not a portfolio of existing planners, that covers the whole language and derives what each answer guarantees from the search that produced it. We prove when the exhaustion of a search shows that no plan exists, and give a relaxation of temporal tasks that proves unsolvability where temporal search cannot. For the compression of durative actions, we give a criterion on the values that actions read, prove that compression is sound, and show that it is not complete, so its failure is never reported as a proof. An independent validator checks every plan. The engineering application is on-line task planning for robots: Kairos is open source, packaged for the Robot Operating System, and the default planner of a widely used planning framework. On 1,756 public tasks from nine suites, Kairos solves more tasks than the strongest evaluated planner of each suite within 100 milliseconds, 1 second and 30 seconds; it proves ten of eleven unsolvable tasks, and handles each of the seventeen language features probed, where no other planner handles more than eight.

replace The Real Cost of Context: Predicting Compute Time and Energy Consumption in LLM Prefill and Decode Phases

Authors: Lars Krupp, Daniel Gei{\ss}ler, Francisco M. Calatrava-Nicolas, Stefan Gerd Fritsch, Paul Lukowicz, Jakob Karolus

Abstract: The integration of Large Language Models (LLMs) into agentic architectures has substantially increased token consumption, raising concerns about their energy footprint. Yet, estimating the energy consumption of proprietary API-based models remains challenging, and existing approaches focus primarily on generated output tokens while overlooking the impact of the large input contexts characteristic of agentic workflows. To address this gap, we systematically profile compute time and energy consumption across a wide range of prefill (input) and decode (output) token lengths. Using generalized linear models, we show that decode compute time and energy scale linearly with output length, whereas prefill compute time and energy exhibit both linear and quadratic scaling with input length. These findings demonstrate that input context becomes a relevant factor for large-context workloads. Furthermore, we show that compute time serves as a reliable proxy for energy consumption when direct energy measurements are unavailable. Our mapping framework enables precise energy estimation and supports more sustainable deployment decisions for agentic AI.

replace An Augmented Lagrangian Approach to Lifted Bellman Linear Programming

Authors: Jongchan Park, Hyukjun Yang, Narim Jeong, Donghwan Lee

Abstract: Temporal-difference critics regress onto a bootstrapped target and usually need a target network. Lifted Bellman Linear Programming (LBLP) instead expresses the in-sample optimal values of offline RL as the solution to a linear program over the dataset. Its neural solver ALBUM relaxes the constraints into hinge penalties with fixed coefficients, and its guarantees are conditions on coefficients chosen in advance. We solve the same program with the method of multipliers: the coefficients become multipliers learned by dual ascent, with one penalty parameter $\rho$. In the convex case the fixed points are the KKT pairs of LBLP, and at every stationary point the applied constraint weights balance the objective exactly. The critic can serve a Gaussian actor, a flow actor, and best-of-N sampling, and for the Gaussian actor we propose an advantage-normalized DDPG+BC objective. With a single $Q$ and $V$ and no target network, our AL-LBLP with best-of-N reaches 60.1\% average success on the 50 state-based OGBench tasks. On long-horizon tasks, it reaches 81.5% on humanoidmaze-giant and 79.8% on antmaze-giant. It does not collapse up to batch size 8192.

replace Small Heads, Strong Decisions: A Controlled Study of Typed-Decision Models

Authors: Ritvik Rastogi

Abstract: In September 2026, TypeSafe announced Jev as the first model in its System One category: unstructured state goes in, and typed, calibrated decisions that software can use directly come out. Jev's architecture and training data are unpublished, but many open typed-decision models have followed. They answer structured questions about a piece of text, such as a yes/no check, a choice among labelled options or a graded score, by reading the text with a language model and scoring the options with a small readout. Each pairs a different readout with a different backbone, training mix and recipe, so it is unclear which design choices matter. We re-implement five readouts and train all of them under one protocol: the same backbone, the same 15 public English training sources, the same recipe with a per-head learning-rate sweep, and the same seed. Our experiments demonstrate that: The complexity of the head does not affect accuracy: on Qwen3-0.6B every readout reaches 81.3-82.0% in-domain accuracy, and a 0.5M-parameter cosine head, which scores each option by its cosine similarity to the question, matches the 27M-parameter joint schema head adapted from Clef in-domain at half its inference time. Reading all of a text's questions from one prompt also matches one prompt per question in accuracy, with 1.6 times faster inference and 2.1-2.5 times faster training on one H100. The backbone's size matters more than either choice: moving from Qwen3-0.6B to Qwen3-4B adds 4 points in-domain and 11 points on held-out sources, while the base, instruct and thinking checkpoints of the same model tie. Checkpoints trained further for embedding or reranking bring no gain. We recommend spending effort on the backbone and keeping the head minimal.

replace Classical Energies Learn to Reason 1,000x Faster than Looped Models

Authors: Benjamin Hoover, Hugh Blayney, James C. R. Whittington

Abstract: Looped models have emerged as a prominent paradigm for reasoning, with successes on simple reasoning puzzles such as Sudoku-Extreme and Maze-Unique. However, these models can be unstable, slow to train, and use many parameters and FLOPs to solve simple puzzles. Descent on an energy not only mitigates these issues but also offers advantages: built-in early stopping, compute that adapts to problem difficulty, fast training without differentiating through a solver, and interpretable weights that learn the constraints of the puzzle. Here we demonstrate this with a learned version of Hopfield & Tank's energy from the 80s, which we term CRAM, solves 99.996% of Sudoku-Extreme and 100% of Maze-Unique test puzzles after 42 ms and 132 ms of training on one GPU, at least 1,000x faster than looped models on these tasks. CRAM also matches baseline looped models at their highest accuracy on Sudoku-Extreme using at least 282x fewer inference FLOPs. Our work suggests two things. First, that energies are an extremely promising direction for training reasoning from data. Second, a caution against using trivially solvable benchmarks like Sudoku and Maze to test the scalability of looped reasoning techniques.

replace Imitation Bootstrapped Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning

Authors: Tony H. Zhang, Connor Mattson, Daniel S. Brown

Abstract: Demonstrations are an effective way to accelerate single-agent reinforcement learning (RL), but extending this idea to multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) is non-trivial as it is difficult and often impossible for a single person to teleoperate multiple agents simultaneously. Round-Robin Behavior Cloning (R2BC) addresses this by letting a single demonstrator control one agent at a time while the others execute their current policies. However, the resulting imitation policies are limited by the quality of the demonstrations. We propose Imitation Bootstrapped Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning (IBMARL), a framework that extends Imitation Bootstrapped Reinforcement Learning (IBRL) to cooperative teams and uses R2BC to train the imitation policies. At each time step, every agent receives one action proposal from its imitation policy and one from its RL policy, and an ensemble of centralized critics selects among the per-agent combinations of the two. A gated imitation penalty, a soft Q-filtered behavior cloning loss, regularizes each RL policy toward its imitation policy only where the critics value the imitation action more, so that after training each agent executes its RL policy alone, without the centralized critics. On four cooperative multi-agent RL benchmarks, IBMARL surpasses its imitation policies $2.6$-$4.2\times$ faster than demonstration-based baselines on two tasks, is the only method to surpass it on the other two, and exceeds the demonstrator itself on two of the four. With real human demonstrations IBMARL still surpasses imitation learning alone, and does so more than twice as fast as the best baseline.

replace How to make a moral agent: sentimentalism and rationalism as lenses on AI

Authors: David Gottlieb, Jared Moore

Abstract: Can AI systems be moral agents? That is, can they undertake actions that can be correctly assessed as morally right or wrong? There is no settled normative criterion of moral agency that applies neatly outside paradigm human cases. Because of the difficulties with a normative account, we instead propose a research program to explore descriptive moral agency. Most basically, we ask, what are the capabilities we humans possess that underlie our moral agency? To do so, we draw on historical scholarship in philosophy and moral psychology to isolate candidate capabilities. Then we suggest refining candidate capabilities so that they are testable in the context of AI systems. Finally, we offer specific empirical recommendations to test AI systems, especially large language models (LLMs), for those refined capabilities. Our approach offers several benefits. Firstly, it may demonstrate whether the behaviors of AI systems are aligned, or in a broad sense desirable. Secondly, the debate may inform policy questions (e.g., what models should be deployed in which contexts?). Thirdly, our approach may complement engineering and design efforts: when capabilities can be precisely characterized, they can inform the development of models. Fourthly, applying our moral reasoning concepts in the new context of AI systems should improve our understanding of those concepts.

replace Controllable Chains of Thought Can Reveal Less, Even Without Evasion Training

Authors: Zheng-Xin Yong, Benjamin Arnav, Miles Wang, Tomek Korbak, Olivia Watkins

Abstract: Chain-of-thought (CoT) monitoring is a promising tool for detecting hidden goals, off-task behavior, and other failures in AI agents. One risk is that models could deliberately shape their reasoning to make it opaque to monitors. We stress-test this risk through persona-control training: supervised fine-tuning on mathematical reasoning traces rewritten in the voices of diverse personas. This induces a broader ability to control CoT, even though training contains no monitor-evasion examples. In covert side-task evaluations, traces in which a gpt-oss-20b model organism completes the hidden side task contain less monitorable evidence than the base model's. Averaged across evaluations and CoT lengths, weighted by side-task-success counts, the CoT monitorability metric g-mean$^2$ falls from 0.94 for the base model to 0.68 for the model organism without a hiding instruction, and to 0.22 when explicitly instructed to hide. In intervention evaluations, explicit hiding often drives monitorability near zero. The effect extends to a Kimi-K2.6 model organism in agentic environments, though hidden objectives requiring extended reasoning or longer trajectories remain more monitorable than simple single-step side tasks. We further discover that targeted transparency prompts can substantially recover monitorability, sometimes surpassing the base model. These model organisms provide a testbed for studying monitorability failures and potential mitigations. Our code can be found on https://github.com/yongzx/cot-control-model-organism.

replace Auditors Struggle to Catch Sabotage In End-to-End Transcripts of AI Safety Research

Authors: Keshav Shenoy, Anthony Zang, Eric Gan, Jacqueline Tay, Amy Ngo, Sebastian Prasanna, Julian Stastny, Vivek Hebbar

Abstract: As AI systems are increasingly used to automate AI R&D, misaligned systems could sabotage research intended to improve model safety and alignment. To stress-test defenses against such sabotage, we use a human expert as a proxy saboteur on AI safety research. We conduct a red-team/blue-team game where a human researcher runs AI safety experiments from scratch and auditors try to tell which were sabotaged and how. Given access to the full research transcript and the ability to run experiments, human auditors mostly fail to distinguish sabotaged from benign research: they achieve an AUROC of 0.59, not significantly better than chance, and fully identify the specific sabotage in only 17% of cases. Despite not being allowed to run experiments, the best frontier models likely outperform human auditors (Fable 5.1: AUROC 0.73, 32% full identification), while weaker models score near or below them. When the saboteur chooses which experiments are attacked, rather than being required to sabotage every question given, sabotage identification drops further. Our results suggest that research sabotage by expert-level agents is difficult to detect with current auditing methods and provide a new setting for studying research sabotage.

replace Think Once, Act Fast: Cost-Effective Recursive Self-Improvement for Decision Model Harness

Authors: Siqi Lai, Haitao Li, Hao Liu

Abstract: Fast System-1 models enable responsive actions but struggle with complex or unfamiliar tasks. Large language model (LLM)-based System-2 provides strong reasoning capabilities, yet repeated invocation incurs substantial latency and computational costs. To bridge this gap, we propose JevRSI, a System-1/System-2 collaboration framework that transforms LLM reasoning into transferable decision support through recursive harness improvement. A Jev-style System-1 model enables low-latency action selection, while an asynchronous System-2 LLM develops a modular harness. The harness integrates procedures, state memory, and world models to provide decision contexts spanning the past, present, and future. Utility-driven retrieval selects modules based on both contextual relevance and their utility on previous tasks. Environmental feedback guides targeted revisions and enables recursive self-improvement. Experiments across three datasets show that JevRSI consistently improves decision quality, matching or surpassing LLM agents in several settings. It preserves low-latency execution with favorable performance-cost trade-offs. Moreover, System-2 development interventions decrease as the harness evolves, amortizing System-2 costs through continuous improvement. Our project is available at https://github.com/usail-hkust/JevRSI.

replace Born for the Task: Rethinking Quantum Chemistry with Foresighted Agentic Computing

Authors: Haozhi Han, Junjie Yang, Ruge Zhang, Zihe Huang, Pengyi Xu, Bo Zhang, Xianyi Zhang, Yunquan Zhang, Ting Cao, Yunxin Liu, Yifeng Chen, Kun Li

Abstract: Quantum chemistry relies on general-purpose software, whose generality leaves many large-scale calculations beyond practical resource budgets. Coding agents make task-specific programs cheap to write, but not efficient ones easy to find: whether a computational strategy is accurate and fast enough becomes clear only after it actually runs at the target scale. We introduce MakoBORN, a foresighted agentic computing framework that synthesizes high performance quantum chemistry programs on demand. MakoBORN anticipates the behavior of each strategy before execution, extrapolating its error from representative small systems and predicting its runtime from the target-scale workload and measured hardware throughputs. Building on these predictions, MakoBORN unfolds the preset schemes of existing packages into independently configurable algorithmic components, selects the fastest strategy within the error tolerance from the predicted accuracy-runtime Pareto frontier, and guides coding agents to compile it with component-level performance predictions. For a Hartree-Fock calculation with 50,024 basis functions, MakoBORN finishes in 1.41 h on a single CPU node, 77$\times$ faster than the estimated runtime of a general-purpose baseline and still 9.8$\times$ faster with development time included. On a single GPU, MakoBORN completes the ubiquitin calculation in 2.1 h, the same order of runtime as the GPU-accelerated package Mako on 64 GPUs, and shortens the B-DNA duplex calculation from an estimated two months to 8.1 h (173$\times$).

replace PhysScope: Agentic Multimodal Physics Reasoning with Grounded Visual Memory

Authors: Haoyang Liu, Kecheng Liang, Xinyu Li, Weixing Chen, Yang Liu

Abstract: Vision-language models (VLMs) can correctly interpret physics diagrams yet apply constraints outside the states or conditions in which they hold, causing premise errors to propagate through subsequent calculations. We introduce PhysScope, an inference-time visual harness that supports self-correction of these scope mismatches without updating model weights. PhysScope enables the model to link physical scopes directly to its visual observations and explicitly constructs derived views to support reasoning. The harness imposes explicit constraints on state distinctions, conditions, and physical scopes during model reasoning, and checks agent-proposed revisions against sources and scopes. Accepted revisions invalidate registered dependents, thereby maintaining scope-aware working memory. On PhysAlign, PhysScope achieves higher solving scores than direct solving across all six evaluated VLMs. With Gemini-3.8-flash, it outperforms Canvas-CoT by 9.34 percentage points and substantially improves solving accuracy on the out-of-distribution (OOD) PhysUniBench benchmark. Ablation studies support explicit scope management and dependency maintenance as design principles for multimodal physics reasoning agents.

replace Why Sub-4-Bit Vision CNNs Collapse and Where Mixed-Precision Recovery Stops

Authors: Michael Schroeder

Abstract: Deep vision models operating in resource-constrained environments depend heavily on low-bit quantization, yet compressing weights below 4 bits frequently induces catastrophic accuracy collapse. While mixed-precision recovery strategies aim to rescue accuracy by selectively retaining high-precision parameters, the fundamental mechanics of where and why these models collapse remain poorly understood. In this study, we systematically analyze the anatomy of quantization collapse across standard Vision CNNs (including ResNet and EfficientNet backbones) evaluated on the full 50,000-image ImageNet-1k validation set. First, we map the exact progression of degradation, demonstrating that collapse is highly non-linear and concentrated in late-stage pointwise projections and depthwise layers. Second, we introduce a structured, multi-step recovery waterfall that evaluates selective integer precision (INT4/INT8) and selective floating-point scales. We show that keeping less than 5% of critical layer activations in 8-bit precision recovers up to 92% of the accuracy lost during aggressive sub-4-bit compression. Finally, we bridge the hardware reality gap by evaluating deployment performance on standard ARM and x86 CPU architectures. We show that while low-bit quantization reduces memory storage requirements, standard runtime software bit-unpacking introduces a 1.48x to 21.5x execution slowdown depending on batch size, highlighting a critical trade-off between model footprint and actual edge throughput. Our findings provide a practical blueprint for navigating the limits of sub-4-bit deployment without sacrificing hardware performance.

Abstract: Federated learning enables collaborative model training without sharing datasets. Authors: Prakhar Mishra, Bhavya Yajush Awasthi, Tanuja, Nishant

Abstract: Small language models (SLMs) can run on commodity hardware and support privacy-preserving, on-premises clinical deployment, but their medical explanations are often short and poorly structured compared with large language models. replace WorldRuler: Interactive World Modeling under Configurable Rules

Authors: Mingliang Zhai, Chuanhao Li, Jiaming Tan, Zhen Li, Yuwei Wu, Yunde Jia, Zian Meng, Yuanyang Yin, Kaipeng Zhang

Abstract: Rules govern how player actions change interactive worlds. Different rules create distinct gameplay experiences within similar scenes and action spaces. For customizable gameplay, generative world models must follow user-defined rules beyond real-world behaviors. However, realizing user-defined rules presents two challenges: Rule-State Persistence and Rule-to-Visual Realization. The latter requires accurately realizing rule-induced changes on designated objects and attributes, particularly when affected objects are unseen. In this paper, we propose WorldRuler, which organizes rule definitions, dependencies, and object states in an executable 3D rule graph and connects rule execution, video generation, and perceptual feedback in a closed loop. The graph supports joint saving and restoration of rules and world states, while dynamic object binding grounds rule-specified entities in visual instances and uses stored states to constrain generation from their first appearance. We further introduce object attribute state replacement and counterfactual reconstruction training, enabling accurate attribute control and compatible appearance and motion changes while limiting interference with unrelated content. We develop an automated data collection and annotation pipeline using GTA-V and Habitat to construct RuleVerse, a dataset of rule-driven interactions with detailed state and event annotations, including cases where rules take effect before affected objects become visible. Extensive experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of WorldRuler in preserving rule-state consistency across saving and loading and accurately realizing rule outcomes on designated objects and attributes.

replace-cross Ensemble Logic

Authors: Guo-Qiang Zhang

Abstract: Ensemble Logic (EL) is a family of modal logics whose modal parameters are terms with quantified variables. We interpret formulas over metric monoids, that is, commutative monoids equipped with translation-invariant metrics. Displacement evaluates a formula at a translated point, while bounded existence and bounded universality evaluate it at some or every point of a window of prescribed length. Quantification over points and lengths allows the same variable to occur in the parameters of several operators. This provides a common framework for temporal and spatial reasoning in discrete, dense and multi-dimensional settings. Our main result establishes a sharp undecidability boundary for satisfiability over the natural numbers: satisfiability is undecidable already for formulas $\exists N\,\exists n\,\Psi$ in which $\Psi$ is built from the letters of a fixed alphabet by conjunction, disjunction, displacement and bounded universality. We prove this by a reduction from Post's Correspondence Problem. We also present results, in part from companion work, that place this theorem in the general theory of EL. These include an atomic-displacement normal form for covariant windows and composition laws for discrete and dense time. The discrete logic admits an embedding of linear temporal logic, defines the accepting runs of every B\"uchi automaton, and has a sound equational calculus. For the rational logic, we show that every fixed formula can be evaluated in polynomial time on interval models in which atomic propositions are interpreted by finite unions of intervals. Finally, we represent timed words over positions and real time and compare two window geometries; with rectangular windows, two shared lengths yield undecidability on finite timed words.

Abstract: Generalizing across time series with irregular temporal structures poses a persistent challenge for universal time series forecasting models. Existing approaches often assume regular sampling or depend heavily on imputation, which limits their applicability in real-world scenarios where irregularities are prevalent due to diverse sensing devices and recording practices. To address this gap, we propose FlexTSF, a flexible and domain-adaptive forecasting model specifically designed for time series with variable temporal regularities. Built upon a decoder-only Transformer architecture, FlexTSF integrates normalized timestamp inputs and domain-specific statistical descriptors through a specialized causal self-attention mechanism, enabling adaptability across domains. Extensive experiments on 16 datasets demonstrate FlexTSF's effectiveness, significantly outperforming existing models in classic forecasting scenarios, zero-shot generalization, and low-resource fine-tuning conditions.

Abstract: Collaborative perception (CP) is a promising method for safe connected and autonomous driving, which enables multiple vehicles to share sensing information to enhance perception performance. Abstract: Learning-based planners are sensitive to the long-tailed distribution of driving data. Common maneuvers dominate datasets, while dangerous or rare scenarios are sparse. replace-cross Rhythm-TTS: High-Fidelity Shortcut Latent Modeling and Probabilistic Rhythm Generation for Zero-Shot Text-to-Speech

Authors: Hieu-Nghia Huynh-Nguyen, Ngoc-Son Nguyen, Long-Khanh Pham Huynh Nguyen Dang, Huy Tien Nguyen, Van Nguyen

Abstract: Zero-shot text-to-speech (TTS) systems are commonly built on either discrete codec tokens or continuous acoustic representations. Discrete-token models benefit from stable sequence modeling and explicit speech factorization, but they ultimately synthesize speech through codebook lookup and codeword composition, which can accumulate errors and impose a quality ceiling on naturalness and speaker similarity. Continuous-representation models can generate fine-grained speech latents directly, but often require heavier in-context modeling and less explicit alignment. This paper presents \textbf{Rhythm-TTS}, a shortcut zero-shot TTS framework that combines the factorizability of discrete codec tokens with the fidelity of continuous latent generation. Instead of using predicted codec tokens as the final acoustic representation, Rhythm-TTS uses them as a semantic-acoustic prior and directly generates holistic continuous speech latents with flow matching, bypassing the discrete-to-latent reconstruction path at inference time. We further address an underexplored dimension of speech naturalness: rhythm diversity across multiple synthesis runs. Motivated by the hypothesis that rhythm variation is largely governed by phoneme duration changes and the occurrence of silent pauses, we introduce probabilistic Duration and Silence Generators to sample dynamic pacing patterns before acoustic generation. Experiments on zero-shot TTS benchmarks evaluate intelligibility, naturalness, speaker similarity, efficiency, and run-to-run rhythm diversity, showing that the proposed shortcut latent modeling and probabilistic rhythm generation improve the fidelity and temporal naturalness of non-autoregressive zero-shot TTS. Code and audio samples are available at our demo page https://rhythm-tts.github.io.

URLs: https://rhythm-tts.github.io.

replace-cross Variational analysis of determinantal varieties

Authors: Yan Yang, Bin Gao, Ya-xiang Yuan

Abstract: Determinantal varieties---the sets of bounded-rank matrices or tensors---have attracted growing interest in low-rank optimization. The tangent cone to low-rank sets is widely studied and underpins a range of geometric methods. The second-order geometry, which encodes curvature information, is more intricate. In this work, we develop a unified framework to derive explicit formulas for both first- and second-order tangent sets to various low-rank sets, including low-rank matrices, tensors, symmetric matrices, and positive semidefinite matrices. The framework also accommodates the intersection of a low-rank set and another set satisfying some assumptions, thereby yielding a tangent intersection rule. Through the lens of tangent sets, we establish a necessary and sufficient condition under which a nonsmooth problem and its smooth parameterization share equivalent second-order stationary points. As applications, we exploit tangent sets to characterize optimality conditions for low-rank optimization and prove that verifying second-order optimality is NP-hard. Moreover, for low-rank semidefinite programs, the Burer--Monteiro parameterization maps fourth-order stationary points of the parameterized problem to second-order stationary points of the original problem. In a separate line of analysis, we investigate variational geometry of the graph of the normal cone to matrix varieties, deriving the explicit Bouligand tangent cone, Fr\'echet and Mordukhovich normal cones to the graph. These results are further applied to develop optimality conditions for low-rank bilevel programs.

replace-cross How Large Language Models Misrepresent American Climate Opinions

Authors: Sola Kim, Jieshu Wang, Marco A. Janssen, John M. Anderies

Abstract: Large language models are increasingly used to simulate public opinion, standing in for survey respondents as "silicon samples." Whether they faithfully represent U.S. climate opinion, particularly at the intersections of race, gender, and ideology, remains largely untested. We prompted six large language models with the profiles of 978 respondents to a nationally representative U.S. climate opinion survey and compared the generated responses with those respondents' own answers across 20 questions. The models compress the diversity of American climate opinion, pulling predictions toward the population mean: less-concerned groups are predicted as more concerned, more-concerned groups as less, and the variance of predicted responses falls, on average, 29% below that of human responses. The compression is intersectional. The models apply one gender assumption, predicting women as more pro-climate than men in every racial group. The assumption matches the direction of the human pattern for White, Hispanic, and Other Americans and runs the wrong way for Black Americans. We claim the direction of that difference rather than its size, since it rests on small cells at the ideological extreme. An audit of gender alone would report that the models understate the gender gap, and would miss that the error changes direction by race, which is what single-variable audits cannot detect. What is at stake is not accuracy alone but representation: whose views a synthetic sample renders legible.

replace-cross The Post-Training Dilemma: Why We Should Rethink the Sequential SFT-RL Paradigm

Authors: Xueyan Niu, Bo Bai, Wei Han, Weixi Zhang

Abstract: This position paper argues that the sequential supervised fine-tuning followed by reinforcement learning (SFT-then-RL) pipeline---the de facto paradigm for large language model (LLM) post-training---has a fundamental property that cannot be fixed by hyperparameter tuning: SFT and RL optimize over nearly orthogonal parameter subspaces, so RL inevitably degrades the capabilities acquired during the preceding SFT phase. Under the Polyak-Lojasiewicz (PL) condition, which is empirically observed for over-parameterized networks, we show that RL increases the SFT loss by at least $\frac{\mu}{2}\mathrm{dist}(\theta_{\mathrm{RL}}, \Theta^*_{\mathrm{SFT}})^2$ for some $\mu>0$. Although the KL penalty can constrain distributional shift, it cannot correct the underlying gradient misalignment between the two objectives. Experiments on Qwen3-0.6B across four tasks confirm the theoretical predictions: on MATH-500, Alpaca, HH-RLHF, and SST-2, the SFT loss increases after the RL phase. We identify the underlying mechanism as persistent near-orthogonality between the gradients of the two objectives, paired with opposing landscape dynamics: SFT flattens the loss landscape while RL re-sharpens it. Based on our analysis, we propose three promising research directions: (1) joint optimization of the two objectives with gradient surgery to resolve conflicting updates; (2) landscape-aware scheduling that terminates RL training at the theoretically optimal point; and (3) characterizing the model scale threshold above which SFT becomes unnecessary.

replace-cross EMA Policy Gradient: Taming Reinforcement Learning for LLMs with EMA Anchor and Top-k KL

Authors: Lunjun Zhang, Jimmy Ba

Abstract: Reinforcement Learning (RL) has enabled Large Language Models (LLMs) to acquire increasingly complex reasoning and agentic behaviors. In this work, we propose two simple techniques to improve policy gradient algorithms for LLMs. First, we replace the fixed anchor policy during RL with an Exponential Moving Average (EMA), similar to a target network in deep Q-learning. Second, we introduce Top-$k$ KL estimator, which allows for flexible interpolation between exact KL and sampled KL. We derive the stability conditions for using EMA anchor; moreover, we show that our Top-$k$ KL estimator yields both unbiased KL values and unbiased gradients at any $k$, while bringing the benefits of exact KL. When combined with GRPO, the two techniques (EMA-PG) lead to a significant performance boost. On math reasoning, it allows R1-distilled Qwen-1.5B to reach 53.9% on OlympiadBench compared to 50.8% by GRPO. On agentic RL domains, with Qwen-3B base, EMA-PG improves GRPO by an average of 35.6% across 7 datasets of Q&A with search engines, including 29.7% $\rightarrow$ 45.8% on HotpotQA, 27.4% $\rightarrow$ 40.5% on 2WikiMultiHopQA. Overall, we show that EMA-PG is a simple, principled, and powerful approach to scaling RL for LLMs. Code: https://github.com/LunjunZhang/ema-pg

URLs: https://github.com/LunjunZhang/ema-pg

replace-cross VLRS-Bench: A Vision-Language Reasoning Benchmark for Remote Sensing

Authors: Zhiming Luo, Hebaixu Wang, Haonan Guo, Jing Zhang, Bo Du

Abstract: Recent advancements in Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have enabled complex reasoning. However, existing remote sensing (RS) benchmarks remain heavily biased toward perception tasks, such as object recognition and scene classification. This limitation hinders the development of MLLMs for cognitively demanding RS applications. To address this, we propose a Vision Language ReaSoning Benchmark (VLRS-Bench), which is the first benchmark exclusively dedicated to complex RS reasoning. replace-cross When and How Much to Imagine: Adaptive Test-Time Scaling with World Models for Visual Spatial Reasoning

Authors: Shoubin Yu, Yue Zhang, Zun Wang, Jaehong Yoon, Huaxiu Yao, Mingyu Ding, Mohit Bansal

Abstract: Despite rapid progress in MLLMs, visual spatial reasoning remains unreliable when correct answers depend on how a scene would appear under unseen or alternative viewpoints. Recent work addresses this by augmenting reasoning with world models for visual imagination, but questions such as when imagination is actually necessary, how much of it is beneficial, and when it becomes harmful, remain poorly understood. In practice, indiscriminate imagination can increase computation and even degrade performance by introducing misleading evidence. In this work, we present an in-depth analysis of test-time visual imagination as a controllable resource for spatial reasoning. We first study when static visual evidence is sufficient, when imagination improves reasoning, and how excessive or unnecessary imagination affects accuracy and efficiency. To support this analysis, we then introduce AVIC, an adaptive test-time framework with world models that explicitly reasons about the sufficiency of current visual evidence before selectively invoking and scaling visual imagination. Finally, to further learn this gating and planning behavior without any annotation of when and how much to imagine, we introduce AVIC-R, which trains the policy via GRPO from QA-correctness rewards and penalties by imagination cost. Across spatial reasoning benchmarks (SAT, MMSI) and an embodied navigation benchmark (R2R), our results reveal clear scenarios where imagination is critical, marginal, or detrimental, and show that selective control can match or outperform fixed imagination strategies with substantially fewer world-model calls and language tokens. Our AVIC-R surpasses strong proprietary baselines including GPT-4o and GPT-4.1 while invoking the world model less often. Overall, our findings highlight the importance of analyzing and controlling test-time imagination for efficient and reliable spatial reasoning.

replace-cross On the Complexity of Offline Reinforcement Learning with Q*-Approximation and Partial Coverage

Authors: Haolin Liu, Braham Snyder, Chen-Yu Wei

Abstract: We study offline reinforcement learning under $Q^\star$-approximation and partial coverage, a setting that motivates practical algorithms such as Conservative $Q$-Learning (CQL; Kumar et al., 2020) but has received limited theoretical attention. Our work is inspired by the following open question: "Are $Q^\star$-realizability and Bellman completeness sufficient for sample-efficient offline RL under partial coverage?" We answer in the negative via an information-theoretic lower bound. To identify additional structure that enables sample-efficient offline RL under partial coverage, we introduce a general decision-estimation framework, inspired by model-free decision-estimation coefficients (DEC) for online RL (Foster et al., 2023b; Liu et al., 2025b). Our framework decomposes offline RL complexity into decision complexity and value estimation error. This allows modular study of both sub-problems. Our result not only unifies existing results (Chen and Jiang, 2022; Uehara et al., 2023), but further improves and generalizes them. On the decision complexity side, our improvement includes: the first $\epsilon^{-2}$ sample complexity bound for soft $Q$-learning under partial coverage that improves Uehara et al.'s (2023) $\epsilon^{-4}$ bound, the removal of the need for additional online interaction in the value-gap setting of Chen and Jiang (2022), and new learnable settings beyond the above two cases. On the value estimation side, we provide a new characterization of the role of Bellman completeness under partial coverage, and the first characterization of offline learnability for general low-Bellman-rank MDPs (Jiang et al., 2017; Du et al., 2021; Jin et al., 2021). The latter is a canonical online RL setting that has remained unexplored in offline RL except for special cases. As a side contribution, our techniques give the first analysis of CQL in the function approximation setting.

replace-cross Learning Object-Centric Spatial Reasoning for Sequential Manipulation in Cluttered Environments

Authors: Chrisantus Eze, Ryan C Julian, Christopher Crick

Abstract: Sequential manipulation in clutter requires selecting which objects to remove and determining how to manipulate them. Learning both decisions jointly can complicate training and make failures difficult to diagnose. We present Unveiler, a modular framework that separates scene reasoning and object selection from action execution through a discrete object-index interface. Its Spatial Relationship Encoder (SRE) selects the best object to grasp, while a separate Action Decoder generates orientation-discretized push-grasp actions. The SRE is trained in two stages: imitation learning from heuristic demonstrations, followed by search-based policy improvement using simulated object removals. This training procedure requires no real-robot training data and allows the SRE to be paired with a different execution policy. We evaluate Unveiler in PyBullet simulation and on a physical Dofbot-Pro. In real-robot experiments, it retrieves two occluded targets in the prescribed order in 90\% of scenes, compared with 20-30\% for the heuristic, GPT-4o, and the imitation-only variant. In PyBullet, Unveiler achieves the highest task-completion rate across all six clutter-density and occlusion conditions, reaching 90.0\% under full occlusion with 6-9 objects, compared with 67.5\% for the heuristic and 60.0\% for ThinkGrasp. These results demonstrate the potential of modular, object-centric spatial reasoning for sequential manipulation in clutter.

replace-cross xAppGen: LLM-driven Natural Language Programming for Agile xApp Development

Authors: Xin Li, Shiming Yu, Leming Shen, Jianing Zhang, Yuanqing Zheng, Yaxiong Xie

Abstract: Traditional RAN systems are closed and monolithic, stifling innovation. replace-cross {\lambda}Split: Self-Supervised Content-Aware Spectral Unmixing for Fluorescence Microscopy

Authors: Federico Carrara, Talley Lambert, Mehdi Seifi, Florian Jug

Abstract: In fluorescence microscopy, spectral unmixing aims to recover individual fluorophore concentrations from spectral images that capture mixed fluorophore emissions. Since classical methods operate pixel-wise and rely on least-squares fitting, their performance degrades with increasingly overlapping emission spectra and higher levels of noise, suggesting that a data-driven approach that can learn and utilize a structural prior might lead to improved results. Learning-based approaches for spectral imaging do exist, but they are either not optimized for microscopy data or are developed for very specific cases that are not applicable to fluorescence microscopy settings. To address this, we propose {\lambda}Split, a physics-informed deep generative model that learns a conditional distribution over concentration maps using a hierarchical Variational Autoencoder. A fully differentiable Spectral Mixer enforces consistency with the image formation process, while the learned structural priors enable state-of-the-art unmixing and implicit noise removal. We demonstrate {\lambda}Split on 3 real-world datasets that we synthetically cast into a total of 66 challenging spectral unmixing benchmarks. We compare our results against a total of 10 baseline methods, including classical methods and a range of learning-based methods. Our results consistently show competitive performance and improved robustness in high noise regimes, when spectra overlap considerably, or when the spectral dimensionality is lowered, making {\lambda}Split a new state-of-the-art for spectral unmixing of fluorescent microscopy data. Importantly, {\lambda}Split is compatible with spectral data produced by standard confocal microscopes, enabling immediate adoption without specialized hardware modifications.

replace-cross Robust LLM Evaluation with Boundedly Bad Judges: A Constrained Maximum Likelihood Approach

Authors: Minghe Shen, Ananth Balashankar, Adam Fisch, David Madras, Miguel Rodrigues

Abstract: Evaluating language models at scale often involves reconciling costly, but reliable, labels (e.g., from human raters) with cheap, but noisy, proxy labels (e.g., from LLM-as-a-judge autoraters). This paper proposes constrained maximum likelihood estimation (CMLE), a likelihood-based approach to large language model (LLM) evaluation that explicitly models the error in the proxy labels via their true-positive and false-positive rates, subject to data-driven constraints. Functionally, these constraints act as an adaptive regularization that restricts the feasible judge error rates, particularly when the available number of reliable labels is limited. In contrast to asymptotically unbiased estimators (such as those based on prediction-powered inference), this formulation accepts a controlled bias under misspecified constraints in exchange for variance reduction relative to various baselines including unconstrained maximum likelihood estimation (UMLE); we characterize this bias--variance tradeoff analytically under point constraints. We identify the regimes in which CMLE outperforms UMLE and prediction-powered baselines in mean squared error. We further extend the framework to heterogeneous settings using a stratified variant involving stratum-specific constraints based on judge confidence. Empirically, on synthetic and real-world LLM evaluation tasks, CMLE achieves up to roughly 9\% lower mean squared error (MSE) than non-stratified baselines, and its stratified variant (SCMLE) achieves up to roughly 30\% lower MSE than the strongest baseline considered, with the largest gains in low-label regimes.

replace-cross Beyond Visual Fidelity: Benchmarking Super-Resolution Models for Large-Scale Remote Sensing Imagery via Downstream Task Integration

Authors: Zhili Li, Kangyang Chai, Zhihao Wang, Xiaowei Jia, Yanhua Li, Gengchen Mai, Sergii Skakun, Zhe Zhu, Dinesh Manocha, Yiqun Xie

Abstract: Super-resolution (SR) techniques have made major advances in reconstructing high-resolution images from low-resolution inputs. In particular, SR has been increasingly developed for satellite-based Earth observation, with applications in urban planning, agriculture, ecology, and disaster response. replace-cross Detecting AI-Generated Videos with Spiking Neural Networks

Authors: Minsuk Jang, Yujin Yang, Hee-Seon Kim, Minseok Son, Younghun Kim, Changick Kim

Abstract: Modern AI-generated videos are photorealistic at the single-frame level, leaving inter-frame dynamics as the main remaining axis for detection. Existing detectors typically handle this temporal evidence in three ways: feeding the full frame sequence to a generic temporal backbone, reducing one dominant temporal cue to fixed video-level descriptors, or comparing temporal features to real-video statistics through a detection metric. These strategies degrade sharply under cross-generator evaluation, where artifact type and timescale vary across generators. On caption-paired benchmark, GenVidBench, we identify two signatures that prior detectors do not jointly exploit: AI-generated videos exhibit smoother frame-to-frame temporal residuals at the pixel level, and more compact trajectories in the semantic feature space, indicating a temporal smoothness gap at both levels. We further observe that, when raw video is fed into a Spiking Neural Networks (SNNs), fake clips elicit firing predominantly at object and motion boundaries, unlike real clips, suggesting that the SNN responds to temporal artifacts localized at edges. These cues are sparse, asynchronous, and concentrated at moments of change, which makes SNNs a natural choice for this task: their event-driven, sparsely-activated dynamics align with the structure of the residual signal in a way that dense ANN backbones do not. Building on this observation, we propose MAST, a detector that processes multi-channel temporal residuals with a spike-driven temporal branch alongside a frozen semantic encoder for cross-generator generalization. On the GenVideo benchmark, MAST achieves 93.14\% mean accuracy across 10 unseen generators under strict cross-generator evaluation, matching or surpassing the strongest ANN-based detectors and demonstrating the practical applicability of SNNs to AI-generated video detection.

replace-cross The Endogeneity of Miscalibration: Impossibility and Escape in Scored Reporting

Authors: Lauri Lov\'en, Sasu Tarkoma

Abstract: An agent's probability report is paid for twice: by a strictly proper scoring rule, which rewards accuracy, and by an approval rule for the decision it triggers. An approval rule that is not affine is known to defeat truthful reporting. We show the conflict is built in: whenever perfect screening is attainable, the welfare-maximizing approval rule is never affine. The distortion is predictable: there is a reserve report at which pretending to be the marginal type costs exactly what approval is worth. Approving every report at or above the reserve sorts agents as if their beliefs were observed, under every strictly proper score and for any belief distribution. So does a continuous rule with one corner; under strict feasibility no continuously differentiable rule does. The binding constraint is steepness, not smoothness: a slope-capped rule sorts perfectly if and only if the cap reaches the critical slope, the steepest chord of the pretending cost up to the reserve; below it the welfare loss is cubic in the shortfall. For the Brier, log and power scores, and whenever the pretending cost is convex up to the reserve, the critical slope has a closed form. The instances are AI-agent oversight and marketplace operation.

replace-cross Behavioral Integrity Verification for AI Agent Skills

Authors: Yuhao Wu, Tung-Ling Li, Hongliang Liu

Abstract: Agent skills extend LLM agents with privileged third-party capabilities such as filesystem access, credentials, network calls, and shell execution. Existing safety work catches malicious prompts and risky runtime actions, but the skill artifact itself goes unverified. We formalize this as the behavioral integrity verification (BIV) problem: a typed set comparison between declared and actual capabilities over a shared taxonomy that bridges code, instructions, and metadata. The BIV framework instantiates this comparison by pairing deterministic code analysis with LLM-assisted capability extraction. The resulting structured evidence supports three downstream analyses: deviation taxonomy, root-cause classification, and malicious-skill detection. Root-cause classification finds that deviations are mostly oversight, not malice: 81.1% trace to developer oversight and 18.9% to adversarial intent, with 5.0% of skills carrying predicted multi-stage attack chains. On a 906-skill malicious-skill detection benchmark, BIV reaches an F1 of 0.949, outperforming state-of-the-art rule-based and single-pass LLM baselines. These results demonstrate behavioral integrity auditing for agent skills at scale.

Abstract: Background: Large language models are increasingly used to assist scholarly peer review, but there is limited evidence about how authors judge their feedback and use it to revise their papers. replace-cross MINTEval: Evaluating Memory under Multi-Target Interference in Long-Horizon Agent Systems

Authors: Hyunji Lee, Justin Chih-Yao Chen, Joykirat Singh, Zaid Khan, Elias Stengel-Eskin, Mohit Bansal

Abstract: Real-world agents operate over long and evolving horizons, where information is repeatedly updated and may interfere across memories, requiring accurate recall and aggregated reasoning over multiple pieces of information. However, existing benchmarks focus on static, independent recall and fail to capture these dynamic interactions between evolving memories. In this paper, we study how current memory-augmented agents perform in realistic, interference-heavy, long-horizon settings across diverse domains and question types. We introduce MINTEval (Long-Horizon Memory under INTerference Evaluation), a benchmark featuring (1) long, highly interconnected contexts with frequently updated information that induces substantial interference, (2) diverse domains (state tracking, multi-turn dialogue, Wikipedia revisions, and GitHub commits), enabling evaluation of domain generalization, and (3) diverse question types that assess robustness to interference, including (i) single-target recall tasks requiring retrieval of a specific target from long contexts, and (ii) multi-target aggregation tasks requiring reasoning over multiple relevant pieces of information. Overall, MINTEval has 15.6k question-answering pairs over long-horizon contexts averaging 138.8k tokens and extending up to 1.8M tokens per instance. We evaluate 7 representative systems, including vanilla long-context LLMs, RAG, and memory-augmented agent frameworks. Across all systems, we observe consistently low performance (avg. 27.9% accuracy), especially on questions requiring aggregated reasoning over multiple pieces of evidence. Our analysis shows that performance is primarily limited by retrieval and memory construction. Furthermore, current memory systems struggle to recall and reason over earlier facts that are revised or interfered with by subsequent context, with accuracy degrading as the number of intervening updates increases.

replace-cross Rethinking Visual Attribution for Chest X-ray Reasoning in Large Vision Language Models

Authors: Guangzhi Xiong, Qiao Jin, Sanchit Sinha, Zhiyong Lu, Aidong Zhang

Abstract: Large Vision Language Models (LVLMs) show promise in medical applications, but their inability to faithfully ground responses in visual evidence raises serious concerns about clinical trustworthiness. While visual attribution methods are widely used to explain LVLM predictions, whether these explanations actually reflect the visual evidence underlying the model's decision is largely unverified, since ground-truth annotations for internal model reasoning are typically unavailable. We address this question for chest X-ray (CXR) reasoning by developing a causal evaluation framework that retains only CXR-VQA samples for which the expert-annotated region is verified, via counterfactual editing, to be causally responsible for the model's prediction. Using this framework across 11 attribution methods, six open-source LVLMs, and two output modes (direct answer and step-by-step reasoning), we find that existing attribution methods often fail to identify the evidence used by LVLMs. To address this failure, we propose MedFocus, a concept-based attribution method that localizes clinically meaningful anatomical regions via unbalanced optimal transport and measures their causal effect on model outputs through targeted interventions. MedFocus produces spatial, concept-level, and token-level attributions and substantially outperforms prior methods, taking a step toward more trustworthy attribution for medical LVLMs. Our data and code are available at https://github.com/gzxiong/medfocus/.
