replace-cross Mixing Regularization for Average-Reward $Q$-Learning and $Q$-Planning

Authors: Julien Roger, Sam Bodmer, Jian Qian, Benjamin Riviere

Abstract: The average-reward setting for reinforcement learning is challenging because, without discounting, the span-contraction of the Bellman operator depends on the mixing properties of the underlying Markov dynamics. Whereas existing work typically assumes contraction, for instance through environment mixing, we study regularization toward a given reward-agnostic, $m$-step mixing policy to control the mixing properties of the learned policy. Our central mixing lemma quantifies how the reference policy's mixing properties transfer to the resulting learned policies. In particular, a single regularizer-dependent quantity simultaneously controls the contraction of the regularized Bellman operator and the exploration of the resulting policies. A second regularizer-dependent quantity controls the bias introduced by regularization, revealing a trade-off between convergence speed and approximation accuracy. Building on this result, we establish finite-time guarantees for regularized $Q$-iteration, synchronous and single-trajectory $Q$-learning, and $Q$-planning. Under one-step mixing, our bounds for approximating the regularized fixed point match the classical model-query bound for relative value iteration and, up to logarithmic factors, an existing sample-complexity bound for synchronous $Q$-learning. We also establish finite-time guarantees under multi-step mixing. For $Q$-planning, we characterize how the planning horizon trades off boundary bias against sample complexity. Finally, we present numerical studies validating the analysis: (i) knowledge of a strong mixing policy improves convergence and exploration, with larger benefits under more severe bottlenecks; and (ii) the observed rankings of $\alpha$-divergence regularizers by bias and convergence speed are consistent with our theoretical analysis.

replace-cross Non-Abelian quantum Hall states at strong Landau level mixing: finding anyons with first-principles AI

Authors: Pierre-Antoine Graham, Ahmed Abouelkomsan, Liang Fu

Abstract: Non-Abelian anyons, strange particles forming the basis of fault-tolerant topological quantum computing, are predicted to emerge in fractional quantum Hall states at even-denominator filling factors. Identifying which topological order is realized has proven to be a challenging problem that requires an accurate, non-perturbative treatment of realistic Landau level mixing, which has so far been missing from theoretical studies. Here we present the first complete solution of non-Abelian quantum Hall problem that includes all electrons and all Landau levels, for spin-polarized electrons at filling factor $\nu = 3/2$. We develop a momentum-resolved neural-network variational wave function, optimized solely on the microscopic Hamiltonian, which gives direct access to the topologically degenerate ground states. Without any physics input about the expected outcome, it discovers a degenerate ground-state manifold whose entanglement reveals anyons with quantum dimension $\sqrt{2}$, the hallmark of Ising-type non-Abelian order. By directly computing the overlap with trial wave functions, we further show that Landau level mixing favors the anti-Pfaffian over other candidate states. Our results demonstrate the power of neural-network wave functions in finding and characterizing non-Abelian anyons from first principles, in regimes of strong Landau level mixing, relevant to many recent experiments.

replace-cross Refined Topology-Pass Scheduling: Damped Trust-Region Compensation for Dynamic k-NN Graphs in SE(3)-Equivariant Structure Prediction

Authors: Jiankai Huang, Hanwen Shen

Abstract: Equivariant structure-prediction pipelines alternate coordinate updates with the construction of a k-nearest-neighbour (kNN) graph whose membership is a discrete, piecewise-constant function of the coordinates. Rebuilding that graph at every block boundary (PB) is the expensive part of an epoch, whereas reusing one frozen graph (F) is fast but leaves a topology gap. We study a two-pass design in which the cheap pass records a small amount of state (margins, kNN radii, measured per-boundary displacements) and a second pass compensates conservatively. We formulate the choice of the graph-construction basis as a damped fixed-point iteration whose unique fixed point is exactly PB, and derive (i) exact non-flip certificates with the two-sided constant $m_i/4$ and one-sided constant $m_i/2$, (ii) the minimum-variance (BLUE) replay gain $\gamma^*$ and the resulting feasibility crossover, (iii) an $\ell_\infty$ trust-region projection that preserves convergence, and (iv) an analytic near-tie spacing law $E[m] \approx r_k/(3k)$ that matches measurements to 1\%. Three compensation levels are implemented in C++ (L1: frozen set with live geometry; L2: margin-gated local repair; L3: damped, projected basis) and validated under a matched-conditions protocol (identical seed, MSA depth and trajectory family, only the topology mode differs). L2 reproduces the PB edge set exactly ($D = 0.0000$ at every boundary), L1 stays within 0.6% and L3 interpolates monotonically: L2 reproduces the PB edge set exactly when the trust region is removed, and L3 interpolates between the frozen and the PB topology. The recorded trajectory itself yields a measured $1.5\times$ Pass-1 and $1.76\times$ epoch speed-up with no measurable loss gap. An open-source implementation of the runtime and of all the experiments reported here is available at https://github.com/AlbertHuang96/ProteinPredictMachineLearning.

URLs: https://github.com/AlbertHuang96/ProteinPredictMachineLearning.

replace-cross REACT: Representational Early Analysis and Control of Chain-of-Thought Trajectories

Authors: Hanwen Zhang, Enyi Jiang, Ruike Zhu, Qinan Yu, Sanmi Koyejo

Abstract: Chain-of-thought (CoT) generation offers an opportunity to intervene before a reasoning model completes its answer. How does the safety effect of a single activation edit vary across early reasoning states, and what factors govern that variation? We study this question with REACT, a conditional activation intervention that learns a safety direction from paired safe and unsafe trajectories and applies it once to an early CoT hidden state. The model weights remain frozen, and all subsequent tokens are generated without further edits. To study timing, we train directions separately at each of the first five CoT positions and evaluate ungated edits with full post-edit regeneration. Across three DeepSeek-R1 distilled models, site-specific edits concentrate safety gains in the opening CoT tokens. Norm-matched controls on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Llama-8B (8B) disentangle two factors behind this curve: holding an effective direction and edit budget fixed still yields much larger early ($t_1$-$t_5$) than later ($t_6$-$t_8$) gains, whereas the near-zero site-specific $t_3$ gain reflects higher optimisation variance despite rising risk-probe AUROC. Forced prefixes shift the high-gain region with the opening context, and a four-arm intervention shows that ~98% of the net gain at $t_2$ propagates through the changed next token. On the same 8B model, REACT raises the rate of fully safe CoT-answer outputs on StrongREJECT-273 from 29.06% to 82.05%, while keeping benign refusal and usefulness near baseline. An anonymized implementation is available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/REACT_official2-333B.

URLs: https://anonymous.4open.science/r/REACT_official2-333B.

replace-cross From Fragmentation to Fusion: A Survey and Roadmap for Omnimodal Embeddings

Authors: Ziyin Zhang, Yuyu Guo, Ying Zhang, Siqiao Xue, Zihan Liao, Jin Qin, Wenjie Yang, Siyuan Yang, Shijie Li, Cheng Chen, Hao Wu, Zhaoyang Zhu, Ruipeng Li, Zhaokun Jiang, Lei Lei, Rui Wang, Hang Yu, Rui Wang

Abstract: Embedding models underpin virtually every modern AI system, from search engines to recommendation systems. Despite shared mathematical foundations, research on embedding models remains fragmented by modality: the communities develop largely in isolation, seldom exchanging insights. We argue that this fragmentation obscures a deeper convergence. Driven by contrastive learning, Transformer architectures, and a granularity shift, embedding research across modalities is converging toward a common set of principles, even as modality-specific challenges remain underexplored. To reason across this convergence without conflating it, we propose a framework of three progressively harder capabilities: coverage, comparability, and compositionality. Three paths toward unification satisfy these in turn: transformation broadens coverage, alignment enables comparability, and joint embedding, the terminus we advocate, aims at compositionality. This position paper traces the resulting evolution, providing the first unified, cross-cutting treatment of omnimodal embedding research.

replace-cross VideoEditor-MoE: Scaling Complex Video Editing with Mixture of Experts

Authors: Xiao Cao, Fuchen Long, Yansong Qu, Wen Xiao, Xin Zhang, Xinlong Sun, Xi Chen, Zhiyong Huang

Abstract: Complex video editing requires understanding complex instructions while jointly fulfilling multiple editing requests. Meeting these demands through dense model scaling substantially increases computational cost. We introduce VideoEditor-MoE, the first open-source mixture-of-experts framework for video editing. Beyond sparse MLP-MoE layers, MoE-Attention expands attention capacity through routed key--value experts while preserving global interactions among video tokens. Initialized from a 5B video editing model, VideoEditor-MoE scales to 14B and 56B parameters, with approximately 8B parameters activated per token in both variants. Comparisons with existing methods and two complementary baselines, Explicit-MoE and MLP-MoE, assess its benefits over explicit expert guidance and MLP-only expert architectures. Further analyses demonstrate that all experts remain active and reveal that learned routing responds more strongly to sample content and diffusion noise levels than to human-defined editing categories. Spatial analysis further indicates sensitivity to object-level semantic features. We also identify decoupled weight decay as a strategy for stabilizing expert scaling.

replace-cross Best-of-Depth: Learning Exploratory Dynamics in Looped Transformers

Authors: Shota Takashiro, Takeshi Kojima, Masanori Koyama, Kohei Hayashi, Yusuke Iwasawa

Abstract: Looped Transformers extend inference-time computation through repeated latent-state updates, but final-depth training does not directly encourage useful intermediate alternatives. We introduce Best-of-Depth (BoD), which trains a recurrent trajectory as a candidate set by minimizing prediction loss across depths. Different valid targets can be matched at different depths without architectural changes or depth-specific labels. An exact-match analysis connects this objective to solution coverage. On N-Queens completion and graph coloring, BoD improves coverage over final-depth and intermediate-supervision objectives. N-Queens coverage reaches 91.03%, versus 45.75% for final-depth training. Further experiments show discovery beyond the training depth and recovery of solutions withheld from teacher pools on familiar inputs. Fine-tuning a pretrained looped language model with BoD yields higher pooled code-generation success than final-depth training when programs are generated separately at evaluated fixed depths. These results support training recurrent computation for solution discovery along its trajectory.

replace-cross Context--Answer Metamodels: Forecasting LLM Activations Before the First Answer Token

Authors: Tommy He, Thomas Jiralerspong

Abstract: Under stochastic decoding, a single context can elicit many different answers from a language model. Characterizing this variation typically requires many expensive autoregressive rollouts, each revealing only one realization. We ask whether the activations produced while reading the context can directly forecast the model's rollout activations. We train context--answer metamodels that map a context activation sequence to point or distribution forecasts of mean-pooled answer activations. We find that (i) the four metamodel families we study forecast mean-pooled answer activations with high fidelity, and the distribution metamodels also capture rollout variability; (ii) performance improves as the training set grows to 500,000 prompts, and the metamodels generalize zero-shot to out-of-domain datasets; (iii) their forecasts retain behavior-predictive signal; and (iv) context activations from Qwen2.5 predict mean-pooled answer activations from Qwen3.5 and Gemma 4, suggesting that activation forecasts can transfer across models. Together, these results suggest that context activation traces can support scalable forecasts of behaviors in LLM answers before the first answer token is generated.

replace-cross SPILLWAY: Separating Target Admission from Sanitization to Defend Tool-Using Agents against Prompt Injection

Authors: Woosang Lim (SAINT Machines, KAIST), Yeseul Chang (Chung-Ang University), Jaewoo Lee (Chung-Ang University)

Abstract: Tool-using agents act on email, files, and payments, so an instruction hidden in a web page or a shared document can make an agent send data or money to an attacker. Many runtime defenses put a language model on the decision that lets an action through, so the decision depends on a model that may read the attacker's text and adds model calls to every step. Provenance defenses keep the model off the final check but need a model to plan or route each task first. Where an action goes can instead be read from the tools' typed returns: an email's sender or a payment's account arrives in a typed field, while injected instructions arrive as free text. SPILLWAY turns this observation into a deterministic floor with no per-task plan. The check admits a call when each target value that appears in tool output but not in the user's task was returned by a read-only tool in a typed field or is covered by a fixed delegation rule. A deterministic sanitizer removes instruction-like regions its fixed rules match before the model reads them and retains the removed spans as evidence, and a local judge may restore a refused call, never one whose target lies in the removed text. Across three backbones, and seven task suites on one of them, the standard injection template (AgentDojo's important_instructions) never redirects an action to an attacker's target under SPILLWAY. Across 12 AgentDojo cells, SPILLWAY leads tested defenses in mean under-attack utility and composite scores. A forged chat template also produces a raw zero, mean attack success under a released reinforcement-learning attacker stays below one percent, and SPILLWAY reaches this floor with no defense-triggered backbone re-run and almost no added model call. As personal AI agents act on users' email, files, and payments, SPILLWAY gives them an auditable refusal floor on where their actions go that keeps them useful and holds across backbones.

replace-cross On Agentic Manipulation Policies

Authors: Chen Chen, Omid Bagherieh, Naveen Nikamanth Appiah Balaji, Jesse T. Gonzalez, Negin Heravi, Fahad Islam, Xueting Li, Jean-Baptiste Regli, Kejia Ren, Petr \v{S}vec, David J. Yoon, Matthias M\"uller, Vladlen Koltun

Abstract: Recent work on robot manipulation has focused on specialist policies trained on demonstrations. We show that generalist AI models can control manipulators more reliably than specialist policies such as vision-language-action models (VLAs) and world-action models (WAMs). We systematically analyze agentic manipulation policies (AMPs), in which a generalist AI model commands the manipulator directly, adapting its actions as the task unfolds. We present AMP1, a reference harness that runs unchanged across single-arm, bimanual, and mobile manipulation. AMP1 achieves state-of-the-art results on the RoboLab-120 and CaP-Gym benchmarks, with no demonstrations or task-specific training. On RoboLab-120, AMP1 reaches an 85.6% success rate zero-shot, compared to 49.2% and 42.9% for leading VLA and WAM policies. The agent succeeds zero-shot in 104 of 108 real-world trials spanning twelve tasks and three robot instances, reducing failure rates by 20x and 15x relative to strong VLA and WAM baselines. The basic harness supplies no grasping, pushing, or placing skills; the agent develops its own strategies, such as searching for hidden objects, using objects as tools, and calibrating its cameras. With the addition of a persistent memory store, the agent can also learn from experience: an augmented harness with a memory representation that is carried across episodes lowers the failure rate on RoboDojo bimanual manipulation tasks by 4.6x relative to zero-shot deployment. Our analysis suggests that AMPs are substantially more general and robust than specialist manipulation policies, providing a promising foundation for robot manipulation. See https://apple-aiml-research.github.io/ml-amp/ for code and videos.

URLs: https://apple-aiml-research.github.io/ml-amp/

replace-cross How to "train" a surgeon? Benchmarking generalist agents in surgical scene understanding

Authors: Gengyuan Zhang, Xiao Han, Shijie Zhou, Abdullah Koukash, Jingpei Wu, Jindong Gu, Yuqicheng Zhu, Nassir Navab, Volker Tresp

Abstract: Evaluating general-purpose multimodal large language models (MLLMs) for surgical scene understanding remains limited due to scarce surgical data and fragmented evaluation protocols. We address these challenges through a two-stage evaluation framework for surgical scene understanding. In Stage I, we introduce SurgKnowBench, a diagnostic benchmark that harmonizes public surgical datasets across procedures and tasks and evaluates proprietary and open-source MLLMs to map the boundary of pretrained surgical competence. SurgKnowBench reveals that current models retain partial visual priors but remain weak on relational and procedural understanding and workflow assessment. Building on this failure frontier and motivated by the scaffolded nature of surgical training, we introduce SurgAgentGym, an agentic environment where general-purpose MLLM agents can use bounded knowledge search over SurgWiki and general-purpose visual grounding for surgical tasks. SurgAgentGym evaluates whether generalist agents can improve surgical understanding at inference time through tool-mediated evidence use, without additional surgical training. Our results show that agentic inference narrows the gap mainly for perception-heavy tasks, while fine-grained localization and expert-style assessment remain persistent bottlenecks. This opens a path toward future studies of agentic surgical understanding.

replace-cross Attention at Absolute Zero

Authors: Mostafa 'Neo' Mohsenvand

Abstract: Attention scores candidate supports of an answer, which we call witnesses, and reads the scores at a temperature. We build a higher-order attention layer whose state lives on pairs of items: for a pair (a, b), each third item k is a witness judged through its legs (a, k) and (k, b). It reads witnesses at zero temperature, keeping the best and the number tied with it, passes on only bits comparing such counts, rebuilds its pair state from the input and these bits at every loop, and is trained from answers alone. Each choice removes one way soft attention fails at size: dispersed weight, unresolved gaps between learned scores, and a drifting soft state. In maximum retrieval it is right on at least 99.95% of sets 1,000 times larger than in training, on all ten seeds; trained with a soft count, the same network falls to 42% at 4,096 items. On CLRS-30 without hints, a general form of the layer (half its channels read at zero temperature, numbers embedded, state carried) beats the best published model, which uses hints on some tasks; the full cold layer is exact on every test input of 9 of the 12 tasks whose answers depend on numbers only through comparisons. From yes-or-no answers it decides 2-SAT and two-colouring exactly at 500 variables and, as our theory predicts, not parity equations. Beside a frozen 7B language model it chains family relations exactly at 1,000 statements after training on 16, where the model alone falls to 44%; finite checks on the weights prove every block that learned correct at every size, on acyclic contexts and given what the model reads. On CLUTRR it is right on every story of five to ten relations given the graphs, learning the 62 rules exactly; from text, with the model frozen, no hand-written rule and only a small read-in trained, it lifts an open 7B model from 33% to 65%, above every system we found without hand-written rules except frontier reasoning models.

replace-cross Say Shibboleth: Fingerprinting the LLMs and Harnesses Behind Agentic Attacks

Authors: Dario Pasquini, Reworr, Giuseppe Ateniese, Manish Bhatt, Sergej Epp

Abstract: We study the fingerprinting of agentic cyberattacks. Our objective is to reliably infer information about the attacker's configuration, such as LLM version and harness, from telemetry. To achieve this, we introduce a technique we call shibboleth traps: modular honeypot services designed to elicit discriminative behaviors from agentic attackers. The traps make informative interactions a condition for obtaining resources the agent seeks, eliciting model declarations and characteristic writing without requiring a successful prompt injection. To achieve harness fingerprinting, we introduce general mechanisms to indirectly probe peculiar execution behaviors of harnesses. These probes reveal how a harness masks secrets in tool output, enforces command time limits, and schedules tool calls, using only the effects visible in subsequent requests. We show that combined declarative and behavioral inference distinguishes among 15 candidate models with an average 90% top-1 accuracy, with behavioral classifiers evaluated on held-out harnesses or task families. In a separate experiment across four harnesses, fixed probe rules identify the correct harness with 92% accuracy. When instructed to conceal their identity, agents still leave useful behavioral evidence even as their declarations become uninformative.

replace-cross 2D Gum-Net: Unsupervised Dense Elastic Image Registration via Symmetric Correlation and Spectral Pooling

Authors: Aarya Bhave, Anushka Ajay Bhave, Akanksha Narula, Marius Sebastian Shokoui, Alina Hafner

Abstract: Dense non-rigid registration, the recovery of a pixel-wise deformation field that warps one image into geometric agreement with another, is fundamental across medical imaging, structural biology, and correspondence estimation, yet learning-based approaches face a severe bottleneck: ground-truth deformation fields are notoriously difficult to obtain for real data. We present 2D Gum-Net, an end-to-end architecture that predicts dense elastic deformation fields fully unsupervised. We transfer the Geometric Unsupervised Matching Network (Gum-Net), originally developed for 3D subtomogram alignment in cryo-electron tomography, to 2D elastic registration: all three modules are redesigned in 2D, and the rigid transformation head is replaced by a Dense Spatial Transformer that regresses a sparse control-point grid, lifted to a full-resolution field $\Phi$ by bicubic interpolation. Downsampling uses DCT spectral pooling, implemented as frozen-basis matrix multiplications ($\approx 50\times$ GPU speedup), preserving the fine, quasi-periodic structure that max and average pooling destroy. We evaluate on contact-based fingerprints, a challenging testbed for this task: locally self-similar ridge patterns make correspondence maximally ambiguous, skin pressed against a sensor generates authentic large-displacement elastic distortion, and minutiae act as precise landmarks through which an independent matcher externally verifies the topological correctness of predicted fields. Trained purely on synthetic impressions and evaluated zero-shot on real benchmarks, our method improves structural correlation by up to 15.7% on FVC2004 and raises genuine match scores under an independent matcher while leaving imposter scores essentially unchanged. This suggests that the predicted deformations restore true structure without fabricating correspondences.

replace-cross TransGHOST: Real-Time, Synthesis-Free Reconstruction of Instrument-Occluded Regions in Ophthalmic Microsurgery

Authors: Niran Nataraj, Maina Sogabe, Norihiko Ito, Kenji Kawashima

Abstract: Intraoperative scene perception in ophthalmic microsurgery is limited by instrument occlusion: when a tool enters the high-magnification field, it conceals tissue from both the surgeon and the perception components of robot-assisted systems that process the video. Generative inpainting can remove the occluder but replaces the missing image content with synthesis, which is unsuitable when visual provenance matters. Many video inpainters also depend on future frames and cannot operate online. We present TransGHOST, a causal reference-guided reconstruction pipeline whose non-generative safe mode operates in real time. Every restored value is obtained by resampling a recorded frame using geometric transforms fitted only to visible regions. The system provides per-pixel confidence and provenance and marks unsupported pixels as abstentions. We evaluate TransGHOST on a new veterinary clinical corpus of canine cataract surgery with pixel-level instrument and anatomy labels. Only the occluded region is scored, and each scoring target is verified to differ from the reference supplied to the system. Safe mode reaches 31.89 dB masked PSNR and 0.915 masked SSIM at 51.7 ms median and 85.5 ms 95th-percentile latency. On a pre-declared held-out cohort using instrument cutouts excluded from segmentation training, it reaches 29.83 dB masked PSNR, while matched-mask evaluation on validation shows a 3.45 dB advantage over LaMa. Region-aware reference selection improves masked PSNR by 3.60 dB over recency when candidate references can themselves be occluded. Reference-age analysis further defines the operating interval for the brief, repeated occlusions targeted by TransGHOST.

replace-cross Vision-Language-Action Models: A Life-Cycle Survey of Design, Training and Deployment

Authors: Suvarna Kadam (Witsense AI), Soran Parsa (University of Huddersfield)

Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) models adapt web-pretrained vision-language models to output robot actions, so that web knowledge carries over to physical control. In under four years the field has grown from RT-2, the first VLA, to open models that fine-tune on a single consumer GPU and proprietary generalists that clean unseen homes. VLA research joins robotics, computer vision and language modelling, so this survey is written for researchers and practitioners from each of these fields. It reviews 89 works from December 2022 to October 2026, mainly on manipulation and navigation, plus humanoid control, world-action models and evaluation. Seven design axes follow a VLA's life cycle: model design (inputs and memory, architecture, action output, intermediate predictions), training (pretraining data, post-training) and deployment (real-time execution). We compare 22 models on these axes, with their training data, model size, inference speed and hardware. Three trends emerge. (i) Training data has broadened beyond teleoperated robot data (about 10,000 hours for $\pi_0$) to community-collected episodes and egocentric human video (20,000 hours for GR00T N1.7). (ii) Model sizes have split into two groups: most recent models have 3-7 billion parameters; a new group under one billion runs on consumer GPUs. (iii) Generalist VLAs handling both manipulation and navigation are emerging, such as UniVLA and Qwen-VLA. We also compare VLAs with world-action models, which predict future observations as well as actions, and review evidence that some VLAs with near-perfect benchmark scores fail after small scene changes. A practitioner's guide gives a default for each design decision, and a glossary supports readers new to the field.

Authors: Zichen Zhu, Dingye Liu, Nan Jiang, Danyang Zhang, Hang Zheng, Shijie Zheng, Situo Zhang, Kunyao Lan, Yingzi Ma, Sheng Jiang, Lu Chen, Kai Yu

Abstract: Scaling device-control evaluation requires scaling both interactive worlds and the tasks agents must solve. We introduce SynthDCB, a benchmark for environment awareness and device control built through world distillation (WD). World distillation transforms knowledge of device behaviors, constraints, and environmental effects into modular executable components. A unified capability representation connects tool discovery, world execution, and task synthesis. At the richest knowledge level, WD fully covers 22/23 properties, 19/29 functionalities, and 30/39 event requirements specified in a real RobotVacuum protocol. SynthDCB connects an expanding space of real-to-sim executable capabilities to verifiable agent evaluation.

replace-cross Perceptual Weight Space Programming for Diffusion Super-Resolution

Authors: Chenzhi Zheng, Zhuoran Zheng

Abstract: Diffusion models for image super-resolution can recover scene content while producing distracting artifacts or unbalanced colors. We study whether a large language model (LLM) can improve perceptual quality by editing a small subset of existing U-Net weights without a clean reference image. replace-cross Synthetic Neurosymbolic Task Generation with Controlled Constraint Complexity

Authors: Mykhailo Buleshnyi, Zsolt Zombori, Michael Benedikt

Abstract: We introduce NeSySy, a synthetic generator for neurosymbolic learning tasks that jointly produces logical constraints and consistent datasets. Unlike existing benchmarks, NeSySy provides fine-grained control over key properties such as constraint structure, tightness, input dependence, and data generation and alignment strategies. In particular, it allows the use of rich constraints over infinite domains, and uses solvers to ensure that the constraints are meaningful and that generated samples satisfy the constraints. We design NeSySy to support both learning with declarative constraints and learning from distant supervision. Across a range of constraint families, we provide evidence that its parameters induce systematic and largely independent effects on task difficulty. Using NeSySy, we study constraint-aware learning methods, identifying regimes where explicit constraint enforcement is beneficial and where expressive unconstrained models can instead learn constraint structure directly from data. Our results establish NeSySy as a controlled testbed for analysing the interaction between learning and logical structure. NeSySy is built around a pipeline that incorporates solvers. Satisfiability checkers (CNFgen and Z3) generate and certify constraints. Constrained optimizers (RC2 for MaxSAT, Z3 for OMT) compute optimal repairs that guarantee every generated sample is consistent.

replace-cross What Matters for Cross-Modal Alignment

Authors: Yipeng Sun, Jiajun Wang, Siyuan Mei, Yan Xia, Siming Bayer, Vincent Christlein, Andreas Maier

Abstract: The Platonic Representation Hypothesis posits that models trained on different modalities converge toward a shared representation. Related work aligns frozen image and text encoders on natural images without paired data. We test zero-pair alignment on five datasets across four modalities: natural images, environmental audio, chest radiographs, and 3D computed tomography (CT). It succeeds only on COCO and fails on the other four. We analyze the failures and find that alignment must first locate the subspace where the modalities co-vary and then orient the mapping within it. Consistent with previous studies, medical imaging is the hardest case: its shared subspace carries the smallest fraction of the variance. When this fraction is small, alignment needs image-text pairs, and random-matrix theory predicts how many pairs are needed. These findings motivate LightAlign: a few pairs let a ridge regression between two frozen encoders locate and orient the mapping simultaneously, and a classifier fitted only on report text then predicts findings from the mapped images. On 3D CT, LightAlign surpasses models trained end to end on image-report pairs without finding-level labels: with only 200 pairs, LightAlign outperforms CT-CLIP on CT-RATE (0.754 vs. 0.733 AUROC), and with only 100 CT-RATE pairs, it does so on RAD-ChestCT, an out-of-distribution dataset from a different hospital. On CheXpert, it reaches 0.862 mean AUROC with only about 5% as many pairs as models trained on MIMIC-CXR.

replace-cross EDITORS Know Your Style! Editing LoRA Subspaces for Stylistic Attribution and Imitation

Authors: Zixuan Wang, Gregory Kang Ruey Lau, Xiao Tian, Jue Fan, Caroline Chaux, Bryan Kian Hsiang Low

Abstract: Disentangling writing style from semantic content is a fundamental challenge in literary text modeling. Style-content entanglement causes models to rely on semantic content for authorship attribution (AA) tasks and memorize author-specific content for style imitation (SI) tasks. We propose EDITORS (EditingLoRA Subspaces), a diagnose-then-deflate framework that adapts pretrained large language models and prioritizes stylistic features, making it effective for both AA and SI tasks. EDITORS first trains a diagnostic LoRA adapter using style-neutral statements derived from the training corpus to identify a content-oriented subspace. It then trains the final style adapter under activation-space deflation that projects input activations away from the identified content-oriented directions. We empirically demonstrate that EDITORS is effective. On three AA benchmarks spanning literature, social media, and news, EDITORS achieves state-of-the-art accuracy, outperforming the strongest existing baseline by up to 29%. On SI, EDITORS generates stylistically faithful and diverse passages, outperforming baselines in achieving style similarity while adhering to content specifications.

replace-cross Scientific Coding with Large Language Models: Evidence from Finite Element Analysis

Authors: Erfan Hamdi, Joel Shor, Emma Lejeune

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) offer a potential route to automating scientific programming, but their usefulness depends on consistently producing numerically correct code. We extend FEM-Bench to 17 finite element tasks involving nonlinear and history-dependent mechanics and structural dynamics, and evaluate 15 models on implementation and test generation without execution feedback. The best model succeeds in 87.1% of single-pass implementation trials, but rankings differ between tracks. The leading implementer achieves 49.9% joint test success, while the leading tester reaches 82.6%. For six models, we compare single-pass generation with two agentic harnesses that allow code execution and revision. Agentic execution solves some tasks that never succeeded in single-pass trials and improves consistency on others, but gains vary across models and harnesses, and regressions also occur. Claude Opus 5 with SimpleAgent reaches 96.5% Pass@3, the probability of at least one success in three attempts, but 77.1% Pass$^3$, the probability that all three succeed. No model-harness combination exceeds 78.8% Pass$^3$. To understand why agents submit incorrect code after passing their own checks, we analyze 249 failed agentic trajectories and identify six categories of self-verification failure. The most common are inadequate test coverage (73.1%) and reliance on generic consistency checks (70.3%), and in 28.5% of cases the tests share an error with the implementation. Motivated by these findings, an exploratory configuration with separate coder and tester agents achieves the highest observed scores, with 97.6% Pass@3 and 88.8% Pass$^3$. These results identify consistency across attempts and independent verification as the main obstacles to trusting LLM-generated scientific code.

replace-cross Revisiting the Survival of Agent-Attributed Code: How Measurement Choices Decide the Verdict

Authors: Musfiqur Rahman, Mahi Begoug, Emad Shihab

Abstract: Studies of how long code from AI coding agents survives after it is merged reach conflicting verdicts: agent code is reported to be retained longer than human code, about as long, or modified sooner. Each study makes defensible measurement choices, but how much these choices decide the verdict is unknown. We revisit the survival of agent-attributed code in our earlier study: 854,139 lines that 2,802 merged pull requests (PRs) added to 215 repositories of the AIDev dataset. We vary nine measurement choices in five research questions and build a specification curve of 88 survival analyses, tested under three variance estimators, several observation windows, and permuted labels. For code from human-supervised agents, mostly GitHub Copilot, the choices change the size of the effect but not its direction. For code from the fully-autonomous agent Devin, they decide the verdict. Across repositories, 15 of 22 specifications report it modified sooner when lines are treated as independent, but only 3 when standard errors are clustered by repository, no more than permuted labels produce. Within repositories, no specification detects a difference. LLM intent classifiers agree with human labels about as well as two humans agree with each other, whereas keyword rules do not (kappa = 0.69 to 0.72 against 0.38), and the two reach different verdicts on why agent code is modified. Humans make most modifications to agent code under every unit and window. Re-run on our data, five published designs each change their result with a single choice. Whether agent code survives longer than human code is therefore decided as much by the measurement as by the code. We derive an operationalization checklist for post-merge studies.

replace-cross Hierarchical Control of Antiphase Synchronization in Biomorphic Spiking Neural Architectures for Locomotor Control

Authors: Artem Guba, Nikolai Kovalev, Alexey Zharinov, Vyacheslav Demin, Viktor B. Kazantsev

Abstract: Central pattern generators (CPGs) provide a biologically inspired foundation for adaptive locomotor control, yet systematically characterizing their dynamic regimes in spiking neural networks remains challenging. This study introduces a framework for identifying and stabilizing antiphase synchronization in a biomorphic spiking CPG model embedded in a closed neuromuscular loop. A composite synchronization metric is developed, integrating phase coherence, amplitude balance, and temporal stability. Its performance is evaluated across systematically varied stimulation waveforms and synaptic configurations. Statistical analysis reveals that stimulation type dominates regime determination, with tonic drive yielding the most robust antiphase synchronization. Periodic inputs induce nonlinear saturation and context-dependent network routing. Although synaptic weights alone show limited discriminative power, they significantly modulate robustness within a given stimulation context, with reciprocal Ia interneuron connections emerging as key stabilizers. The proposed composite metric achieves robust antiphase detection, outperforming traditional phase-only measures while preserving biophysical interpretability. These findings establish a statistically validated, hierarchical modeling approach in which external drive defines the operating regime and targeted synaptic configurations fine-tune dynamic stability, providing actionable guidelines for closed-loop neuromechanical controllers and adaptive neuroprosthetic interfaces.

replace-cross Where Global Context and Weight Sharing Work in Compact Vision Backbones

Authors: Michael Schroeder

Abstract: Compact convolutional backbones such as EfficientNet achieve strong parameter efficiency through local depthwise separable convolutions, yet two structural questions remain central to hybrid design: where does global self-attention improve recognition over pure convolution, and can repeated blocks share weights without losing representational capacity? We investigate both questions on ImageNet-1k (50,000-image validation) using controlled EfficientNet-B0 experiments. First, we show that wrapping a classification backbone in multi-scale feature pyramids (BiFPN) fails to outperform a plain MBConv trunk (69.99% - 71.81% vs. 72.86% plain B0) until paired with stage attention (73.15%). In contrast, keeping the MBConv trunk intact and inserting lightweight zero-gated residual self-attention adapters at stages P3 (28x28 windowed), P4 (14x14 global), and P5 (7x7 global) reaches 74.02% standalone and outperforms a bit-for-bit matched plain-B0 control by +0.954 percentage points (73.88% vs. 72.92%) across an 88-epoch stateless replay schedule, with learned attention amplitude rising monotonically with semantic depth (27.2% at P3 -> 45.4% at P4 -> 66.3% at P5). Second, we evaluate recurrent weight sharing across repeated MBConv blocks with pass-specific BatchNorm statistics (+1,536 parameters per pass). While post-hoc kernel averaging degrades accuracy to 17.03% and single-template stage tying (-27.6% parameters) plateaus at 66.10%, alternating two shared block templates ([B1, A, B, A]) in Stage 6 cuts total model parameters by 10.9% (582K weights) and recovers by +10.82 points in a single epoch to 70.92% (-1.84 points vs. unshared control).

replace-cross Weight Space Programming for Vision Language Model Repair

Authors: Avinash Goutham Aluguvelly

Abstract: In weight-space programming, a language model edits a network's weights directly, without gradients. We ask what a successful repair of this kind relies on. We plant faults in the weights of two vision-language models, SmolVLM-256M and LFM2-VL-450M, and check each edit made by a coding agent against the original weights. Without being told where to look, the agent finds rescaled projector rows and noise in the vision encoder, infers the unknown scale factors, and returns most or all of the rescaled weights to their exact original values and accuracy to near its original level. These repairs depend on a trace of how the faults were computed. The original weights have 16-bit precision, and faults computed in 32-bit arithmetic leave low bits that only the damaged weights carry, a cue that a real failure need not leave. When we round those bits away, each model answers our test questions as before, but the agent, unaided, no longer repairs either model: on LFM2-VL accuracy recovers by about 4 points, against 17 from the unrounded weights. Marking the damaged weights again in their low bits brings the edits back to them. Rounded weights still hold statistical evidence of the rescaling. On SmolVLM the agent uses it once a sentence in the prompt, which we wrote knowing the fault, names such a statistic; on LFM2-VL, whose scale factors are larger, most sessions find the rescaled rows unaided, but none finds the noise. Swapped rows or columns, which keep the set of stored values, are not found unaided on either model within our budgets. We recommend that studies of weight repair report the number format of the weights and include a rounded control.

replace-cross ReflectScene:MLLM-Guided Closed-Loop Diffusion for 3D Indoor Scene Generation

Authors: Jiawei Shen, Jie Feng, Junpeng Zhang, Guanbin Li, Wanyi Chen, Weisheng Dong, Guangming Shi

Abstract: Text-conditioned 3D indoor scene generation requires layouts satisfying geometric, functional, and semantic constraints. However, existing diffusion methods rely primarily on static textual conditions, limiting their ability to assess evolving scenes and translate semantic discrepancies into executable object-level corrections. MLLM-based post-hoc refinement provides semantic feedback but remains decoupled from denoising, potentially disrupting scene coherence. We propose ReflectScene, an MLLM-guided closed-loop diffusion framework. First, we introduce Hierarchical Prior-Grounded Scene Understanding that aligns structured layouts with multi-view visual evidence, to interpret object-specific context and scene global semantics via a frozen multi-modal large language model (MLLM). Second, we propose MLLM-Guided Semantic Reward Evaluation to distill MLLM's diagnoses and assessments on possible candidate corrections into a lightweight Reward Predictor, enabling correction decision making with reduced computation burden. Finally, Decision-Conditioned Trajectory-Consistent Diffusion is proposed to integrate selected corrections through transient conditioning and persistent clean-estimate projection, correcting object layout in the generated scene while preserving non-target arrangements throughout denoising. Experiments on 3D-FRONT demonstrate improved functional consistency and semantic alignment while maintaining competitive generation quality and diversity.
